## 2. Acknowledgement 

This report provides an independent, evidence-based evaluation of the SBCC Cells in six states in India (20172021). The UNICEF India Country Office (ICO) engaged PricewaterhouseCoopers India (PwC) to undertake the evaluation. As a part of this evaluation extensive consultations were undertaken to gather insights and inputs from a wide range of stakeholders, including government and implementing partners at the national, state and district level. 

The evaluation team from PwC acknowledges the contribution and support from all those who contributed in the evaluation. We received continuous support and guidance from UNICEF ICO in all the stages of the evaluation. Their support was valuable for determining the evaluation design and in organizing interviews with the key personnel. Additionally, we received their support to access state specific information/ documents.  We would like to express our gratitude and appreciation to the following individuals from UNICEF team Mr. Atishay Mathur, Research & Evaluation Specialist, Dr. Nidhi Pasi, Research & Evaluation Specialist, Mr. Siddharta Shreshta, Chief SBC, Ms. Alka Malhotra, SBC Specialist, Ms. Cheshta Gulati, SBC Consultant, and ERG members Catalina Salazar Silva, Dr. Vishal Singh, Associate Professor, Communication, SIHFW-Rajasthan, Dr. Kalas Baviskar , DDHS,IEC, Maharashtra  for their extensive support **.** Their detailed comments on the inception report and draft evaluation report have helped to strengthen the evaluation report. ## 3. Executive summary 

## **Background** 

Information, Education, and Communication (IEC) is a process of adequately supporting information and education to people to advance healthy behaviors which are influenced by several factors such as the local context, family, and the community – factors that are crucial to an individual’s growth[1] . 

The concept of Social and Behavior Change Communication (SBCC) which is an evidence-based, consultative process of addressing social attitudes, knowledge and practices has evolved from earlier models of IEC done through identifying, analyzing and providing communities and individuals with relevant information through welldefined strategies. SBCC aims to make changes in knowledge, attitudes, and practices and has emerged as a more comprehensive approach than IEC. While IEC involves use of wide range of channels and material to pass on fact based instructional messages to the individual or community, SBCC acknowledges underlying socio-economic factors that affect individual behavior and, uses robust approach to facilitate improved social behavior and mobilize targeted populations for change. Various countries and international organizations across the globe have begun investing in SBCC activities to empower individuals and communities, and lower structural barriers that hinder people from adopting positive practices and societies from becoming more equitable, inclusive, cohesive and peaceful.[2] 

Over last two decades, India’s growth has contributed phenomenally to global human development. However, despite making considerable progress over the years, it continues to face challenges with reproductive health, child health and nutrition.[3] SBCC interventions have increasingly been seen as crucial contributor towards addressing social and cultural barriers in health programmes and enhancing positive behavior towards health and wellbeing of oneself and community, as a whole.  In this light, UNICEF has been working actively along with Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW) and state governments to strengthen the health system institutional structures for SBCC. Behavior change communication has been inculcated as a part of the larger Health Systems Strengthening program approach. 

In 2013, a national behavior change communication (BCC) strategy for RI was developed with UNICEF’s support. UNICEF also extended its support to GoI towards institutionalization of SBCC Cells and transformed communication focus from IEC to demand generation through behavioral change. In 2014, Global Alliance for Vaccines and Immunization (GAVI) provided support to this by revamping and revitalizing existing IEC/BCC institutions; or setting up new ones within the state institutions. The SBCC Cells were equipped with trained human resources and budgets that directly supported the State Immunization Division and played a significant role in planning and operationalization of communication interventions for flagship interventions such a s Mission Indra Dhanush and new vaccine introductions. Under the GAVI HSS 2 UNICEF is mandated to engage and facilitate the process of SBCC Cells with the sustainability considerations as stated in the programme document, which outlines that the cells must be fully state government owned and operated. 

## **Objective:** 

With the GAVI programme gradually drawing to a close, this UNICEF SBCC evaluation is intended to provide critical programmatic insights into what has worked, what has not worked and key learnings from the sample states included in the evaluation. Similarly, the findings of the evaluation are expected to support strengthening of SBCC cell functioning, and ongoing program initiatives aimed directly at maximizing utilization of public services.  Based on the findings of the evaluation, UNICEF aims to advise the MoHFW and other public administrative bodies on the effectiveness and sustainability of institutionalizing SBCC Cells. 

## **Methodology:** 

PricewaterhouseCoopers India (PwC) was engaged by the UNICEF India Country Office (ICO) to undertake this evaluation study. The geographic scope of the evaluation state specific (six states, namely, Odisha, Madhya Pradesh, West Bengal, Uttar Pradesh, Telangana, and Assam). A purposive sampling approach was used in district selection across sample states. Further, details on the rationale used for district selection has been included in the methodology section of the report (3.4.2). The SBCC Cells were assessed on the OECD DAC criteria to understand 

> 2 https://www.unicef.org/social-and-behaviour-change 

> 3 https://www.unicef.org/india/children in india 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

9 

the milestones reached by these cells in terms of relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, coherence, and sustainability considerations within the period of evaluation that is 2017-2021. 

The evaluation followed the health systems strengthening approach which was also a part of the SBCC systems strengthening, and which included SBCC workforce, service delivery, financing, leadership, governance, information, and knowledge management. Gender and equity lens was adopted as cross-cutting themes across all evaluation objectives. The data collection for the evaluation was conducted during November 2022 to January 2023. 

The evaluation was mainly conducted by the way of qualitative interviews and FGDs. Additionally, certain identified aspects were verified through observations such as availability of IEC material (Flyers, posters, media clips on social media), display of IEC material at the SBCC cell, and health centers etc. Similarly, aspects related to routine practices were assessed through observation and record reviews. 

## **Key findings** 

The main findings are described for each evaluation criterion and structured around the key evaluation questions that guided the evaluation. 

**Relevance** : The SBCC Cells aligned with the national and state priorities and other programmes, however the degree of alignment varied from model to model. The cells-initiated activities enhanced the reach and adherence of the programmes and interventions at grass root level. Even during the pandemic cells responded pro-actively and developed strategies, designed materials and planned activities to ensure right information is provided to people in efficient and effective manner. 

**Effectiveness** : SBCC cells have been only partially successful in achieving their desired goals and objectives. Lack of systematic structure, scarcity of financial and human resources, limited scope of creativity negatively impacted the performance of the cells. Although the cells were established to lead the communication change interventions but majority of them ended up being supportive unit to the state’s IEC activities. 

**Efficiency:** SBCC cells across the states (except Telangana) did not have a say in allocation of financial resources for the SBCC activities. This limits the cells’ efficiency in delivery due to lack of adequate funding for monitoring, research and evidence generation and other essential aspects of SBCC. Due to various reasons like vacant posts, retired / transferred personnel, lack of dedicated communication cadre etc. coupled with lack of dedicated financial allocations leads to reduced efficiency of cells to function within planned timelines. 

**Coherence** : In most of the states, the cells had good coordination and convergence with state government. Cells are used to consistently provide government support for implementation of communication strategies at state and district level. However, intersectoral convergence was missing due to which cell were not able to achieve its goals and objectives in effective and efficient manner. It was also observed that cells failed to utilize the potential of other development partners and NGOs in creating a difference and initiating social behavioral change. 

**Sustainability** : The SBCC cells work is well integrated into the programming of the departments they are housed under. However, they have a considerable scope of scaling up and integration in the public health system of the country and states. The government (at the state and district-level) have been reported to have limited capacity to adequately carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently at present due to limited technical knowledge / expertise and dedicated human resource and financial allocations. Collaborative efforts of all stakeholders would enhance the pool of resources and increase the reach of the cells and help in its transition. 

**Conclusio:** The SBCC cells program was initiated to help state government departments to transition from IEC to SBCC. Though this transition is not yet complete for any of the six states evaluated in this study, the inception of the cells has got the ball rolling in the right direction. The state and district level officials are now familiar with the term ‘SBCC’ and are aware of its basic conceptual definition and how it differs from IEC. They are also aware of the novel methods being used in SBCC such as miking, interpersonal communication, leveraging key influencers, etc. The SBCC program has contributed to health systems strengthening in these six states by bringing about a change in the way health, ICDS and PRI officials approach and engage with community members. 

In conclusion, it can be said that the SBCC cells program **kickstarted the transition of communications activities from IEC to SBCC** . The program's contribution has been significant and in many of the states been the first SBCC intervention initiated. Though the progress has been slow over the past programmatic cycle, it is important to understand that to bring about a change not just in methods but in the approach, understanding and attitude towards communications is a mammoth task. The very principles of SBCC can be applied here to see the slow progress not as a sign of inefficiency and ineffectiveness of the program, but rather as a **work in progress status for the cells** . The cells require guidance and support for at least one more programmatic cycle whilst they figure out their transition strategy to move from being UNICEF supported to completely government owned. **We strongly suggest UNICEF to continue its investment** and support in the program during this period. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

10 

## **Recommendations:** 

The recommendations have been formulated based on the findings which were analyzed through primary and secondary data. The recommendations are divided between those which are common to all the cells across all 3 categories and those which are specific to each category, and are formulated specific to the two main stakeholders, i.e., the government and UNICEF. Broadly, the recommendations are focused on addressing the need for establishment of district-level cells in all the six states, continued need of technical assistance and aid in handover process from UNICEF, capacity building and skill development of health officials at the state, district and block level, and the need for increased agency for cells to lead SBCC programming and have a greater say in budget and timeline planning. We also recommend, a similar evaluation to assess the progress of the transition of the state and decide future course of action at the end of the next programmatic cycle of five years.

## Introduction 4. 

India emerged as world’s fifth largest economy by Nominal GDP and third largest by PPP in the year 2022[4] . Along with development of extensive tangible infrastructures there has been emphasis on change and progress at grass root level. Social building blocks and interventions in the form of Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan for increasing access to education, midday meal for nutritious food to children, Mission Indra Dhanush for Routine Immunization (RI), comprehensive primary care through Health and Wellness Centers (HWCs) and other measures for ensuring maternal and child health have been implemented to ensure a community where people are healthy (not merely illness free) and take onus of their health and wellbeing. In health sector, India has made substantial progress over the past decades. It is evident from the fact that, life expectancy at birth in India has grown from 45 years in 1960s to 70 currently.[5 ] 

India is presently in a state of demographic and epidemiological transition. It bears triple burden of diseases namely, the infectious diseases; the noncommunicable diseases (NCDs) linked with lifestyle changes; and emerging epidemics and pandemics[6] . Despite making considerable progress over the years, it continues to face challenges with reproductive health, child health and nutrition[7] . Indicators, like, Maternal Mortality Ratio in India being just below 100[8] , Infant Mortality Rate being 28 and Under 5 Mortality rate being 32[9] suggest that India still has a long way to go to achieve SDG targets[10] . It is essential to understand that better health and wellbeing of individuals cannot be established only through development of curative health services. Various interrelated social and behavioral determinants of health that contribute towards morbidities and mortalities need to be taken into consideration as they pose tougher challenges to the public health system. In fact, the epidemiological transition is fueled by these social and behavioral determinants of health in addition to other risk factors. 

Nutrition is a crucial factor for health and development. Similarly, it is fundamental for improvement in infant, child and maternal health. It also leads to stronger immune systems and contributes to safer pregnancy and childbirth and lowers the risk of non-communicable diseases[11] . Over the years, India continues to face a double burden of nutritional problem. It can be seen through NFHS-5 data that, there is sub-optimal progress in health and nutritional status in the population. In 2019 –21, only 11% of breast-feeding children aged 6 - 23 months (about 2 years) receive an adequate diet, which was 8.3% in 2015-16 (NFHS 4). On the other hand, among the non - breastfeeding children the number has gone down to 12.7%, from 14.3% in 2015-16. When we consider children aged under 5 years, 35.5% are stunted (height-for-age), 32.1% are underweight (weight-for-age) and 19.3% are wasted (weight-for-height). Additionally, percentage of anemic children among children aged 6-59 months (about 5 years) has increased from 59% to 67% between 2015-16 to 2019-21. Malnutrition and anemia in young age sustains throughout the life span of individuals, especially women. Among the women aged between 15-19 years, 59% are anemic and among pregnant women aged 15-49 years 52.2% are anemic. On the other hand, obesity is also increasing its prevalence in India. According to NFHS – 5 data, 24% women and 23% of men in India are overweight or obese. Additionally, 57% women and 48% men have high risk waist-to-hip ratio[12] . The double burden of high prevalence of undernourishment and increasing prevalence of obesity in a country reiterates the need for social and behavior change interventions. 

The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), in particular, SDG 3 – Good health and well-being, and SGD 5 – Gender Equality cannot be achieved without bringing in behavioural change at all levels, combined with structural policy implementation. While practitioners of SBCC in India at state and district levels are tackling various challenges related to health (maternal, infant, and child mortality, etc.,), gender biases and equity and participatory approaches of practitioners, SBCC representatives on field and within communities, they can engage more effectively for partnerships through greater resource utilization and wider impact of SBCC interventions.  The SBCC program falls within the ambit of SDG 3, which focuses on the following goals: 

- Reduce the mortality rate from noncommunicable diseases.

- Reduce communicable diseases. Improve mental health and well-being and prevent substance abuse. 

- Create and improve health systems and access to essential medicines and vaccines. 

- Improve healthcare access for vulnerable populations, including women, child ren, and the elderly. 

- Mitigate the impact of health emergencies such as pandemics and natural disasters. 

The integration of SBCC programming in health programming directly addresses points three, four and five which are creating and improving health systems and access to essential medicines and vaccines, improving healthcare access for vulnerable populations, including women, children, and the elderly, and mitigating the impact of health emergencies such as pandemics and natural disasters. Further, through ensuring wider access to health messaging, the SBCC program also is working towards achievement of points one and two. Further, some important specific targets of SDG 3 include reducing maternal and child mortality, tackling infectious diseases, and ensuring universal access to sexual and reproductive health and rights which are also a primary focus of the SBCC program. 

There has been well established iterative relationship between education and health. Literature shows a positive association between education attainment and health outcomes[13] . Education is therefore an important social determinant of health. Over the years, the country has significantly improved access to education. However, a substantial proportion of the population lacks formal education which negatively impacts their health and wellbeing seeking behavior. According to NFHS 5 (2019 – 21) data, in India, 72% (69% in 2015-16) of the female population, who are aged 6 years and above have ever attended school. This number reduces to 41% the indicator 10 or more years of schooling is considered. Similarly, it was seen that only 50% of men completed 10 or more years of schooling. Lack of proper education can lead to lower acceptability and adherence towards preventive interventions and promotive health practices. It is evident from the fact that, only 58% of mothers in India had at least 4 antenatal care visits and only 44% of women consumed iron folic acid for 100 days (about 3 and a half months). Such health-related behaviors from families continue post birth of child as well. Today, even after extensive implementation of RI interventions, close to one – fourth of children aged 12-23 months (about 2 years) are not fully vaccinated.[14] UNESCO’s Global Education Monitoring Report highlights that the attainment of higher levels of education among mothers is positively related to children’s nutrition and vaccination rates, which contributes to decrease in preventable child deaths, maternal mortality[15] . 

Along with education, lower demand of health and wellbeing services prevail due to certain supply and demand side barriers. People find visiting health facilities or Anganwadi for preventive and promotive health services difficult and time consuming. As preventive and promotive health measures do not provide immediate outcomes, underutilization of these services is observed. The statistics related to ante natal visits and vaccination provide evidence for the same. Additionally, even when services are brought at doorsteps communication barriers between service provider and beneficiary can curtail its acceptance. According to NFHS 5 data, only 24% of nonuser females accepted that health workers had ever talked to them about family planning and its methods[16] . The challenges listed above are crucial determinants towards ensuring the health and wellbeing of individuals and society. On one hand, lack of education and awareness, distance from health facility, social beliefs and myths, attitude towards health workers, commutation and communication barriers negatively impact health seeking behavior of people leading to poor adherence and acceptability of preventive and promotive initiatives whereas on the other hand, malnutrition, lack of WASH facilities increase the susceptibility of the people towards numerous infections and ailments. 

Although, across the country infrastructure is being built and updated (1.5 lakh HWCs operationalized till December 2022[17] ) and health workforce have been deployed across the country to ensure consistent service delivery and innovative interventions implemented and over the years, governments at Union and state level have also invested financial and human resources for Information, Education and Communication (IEC) activities, but governments also need to focus on skill development and training of existing health workforce to enhance their inter-personal and communication skills. Additionally, public health system of India requires further recruitment of health workers for reduced workload of existing personnel and ensure better productivity. According to rural health statistics 2020 -21, out of the total requirements, 20.6% posts are vacant and there is shortfall of 30.8% of HW 

> 13 Raghupathi, V., Raghupathi, W. The influence of education on health: an empirical assessment of OECD countries for the period  1995– 2015. Arch Public Health 78, 20 (2020). https://doi.org/10.1186/s13690-020-00402-5

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

14 

(F)/ANM at PHC level[18] . The need of the hour is not only to undertake initiatives to deliver better quality services in an effective manner by resolving supply side constraints but also encourage people to access services by initiatives to tackle demand side constraints. 

Effective and efficient communication pushes individuals and communities to adapt to progressive health and hygiene supportive behaviors. Under the Swatch Bharat Mission, inter – personal communication initiatives contributed significantly to IEC coverage under Swachh Bharat. The SBM was successful in delivering the message of safe sanitation practices to hundreds of millions of Indians through a unique ecosystem approach. This approach to catalyzing mission focused IEC activities led to a paradigm shift in how India approaches awareness and behavior change across sectors[19] . According to NFHS 5 data, in 2019-21, 70% of the population were living in households that use an improved sanitation facility and 77.3% of the women aged 15-24 years use hygienic methods of protection during their menstrual period. When compared with NHFS 4 data, the situation has certainly improved from 49% and 58% respectively. Enhancement of positive behavior towards cleanliness, sanitation may be attributed to effective communication by various stakeholders to ensure people initiate social and behavior changes related to health and hygiene as seen in Swachh Bharat campaign which has enhanced focus on SBC component[20] . Similarly, there has been enhanced focus on SBC using a 360-degree approach to promote safe and hygienic menstrual health practices in recent years which might have resulted in improvement of said indicator over the years[21] . 

In beginning of 2020, the world began its combat against COVID 19. Over the course of the three years, policymakers and health systems witnessed variations in infection and mortality rates regional, state, national and internal levels which pushed for dynamic and adaptive public health and social measures. The impact of COVID19 has overwhelmed some of the most robust healthcare systems.[22] This pandemic was much more than a health crisis. It was a communication, information, education, and behavioral crisis. It was identified that behaviors will drive this crisis, determine its trajectory and possess the power to mitigate the risk of the pandemic. Health systems across globe evolved and SBCC activities were initiated to curtail the impact of pandemic. The Indian government also considered Risk Communication and Community Engagement (RCCE) to be ‘an integral part of a public health emergency response at every level’ and had a detailed action plan for its use during the COVID 19 pandemic.[23] SBCC activities initiated by various stakeholders in India led to adoption of positive health and wellbeing practices which contributed towards effective and efficient management of the pandemic. 

## 4.1. Background and context of intervention 

Social and Behavior Change Communication (SBCC) becomes a center point of demand generation for health systems strengthening which in turn uses communication to change behaviors including service utilization and promote social change by positively influencing knowledge, attitudes, and social norms. UNICEF’s engagement with the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW) and state governments aimed to strengthen the health system institutional structures for SBCC. In 2013, Global Alliance for Vaccines and Immunization (GAVI) committed a support of USD 107 million for the first phase of its Health Systems Strengthening program in India. This fund supported the government’s intensified efforts focusing on overall health system improvements. Among others, the increase in demand for RI through a national behavior change communication (BCC) strategy was a priority area. This RI communication strategy for the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare was developed in 2013 with UNICEF’s support. Behavior change communication was part of the larger HSS1 approach. UNICEF also extended its support to GoI towards institutionalization of SBCC Cells and transformed communication focus from IEC to demand generation through behavioral change. Incorporation of Social and Behavior Change Communication (SBCC) methods was envisaged to act as a fulcrum across stages of programming and planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and evidence generation. In 2014, under GAVI HSS 1, effort was channeled towards revamping and revitalizing exiting IEC/BCC institutions; or setting up new ones within the state institutions if no set-ups for communication existed. By 2016, MoHFW had a comprehensive SBCC strategy in place to guide state and national programs on health and nutrition. Methods included in this strategy were IPC, community mobilization, mass media, and ICT. 

> 18 https://main.mohfw.gov.in/sites/default/files/rhs20-21_1.pdf 

> 19 https://jalshakti-ddws.gov.in/sites/default/files/201906_SBM_IEC_Whitepaper.pdf 

> 20 https://jalshakti-ddws.gov.in/sites/default/files/201906_SBM_IEC_Whitepaper.pdf

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

15 

The SBCC Cells were equipped with trained human resources and budgets that directly supported the State Immunization Division and played a significant role in planning and operationalization of communication interventions for flagship interventions such as Mission Indra Dhanush and new vaccine introductions. The intent of the SBCC Cell was to strengthen programme planning and implementation through evidence based SBCC focused approaches. It is important to note that few states of India such as Odisha, West Bengal and Madhya Pradesh already had institutionalized IEC/BCC/SBCC cells even before the allocation of GAVI HSS1 fund in 2014. With funding from GAVI HSS 1, and subsequently from HSS 2, the focus was to systematically integrate SBCC in programme conceptualization. SBCC was positioned as a catalyst to augment the results of RI programming at the state and district level. The states of Assam, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh, and Odisha were the fore runners in setting up and operationalizing the cells. Adopting a bottom-up approach, selected states such as West Bengal, Uttar Pradesh, Odisha, and Madhya Pradesh focused on institutionalizing the SBCC Cells at the district level that supported resource mobilization from different yet connected line departments. On the other hand, the SBCC cell set up in Andhra Pradesh serves the three states of Andhra Pradesh, Telangana and Karnataka and focuses on themes beyond RMNCH+A and RI such as RCCE and ODF. It is known as the Communication Resource Unit (CRU) in these states. 

Overall, the model was flexible in meeting the need and context for the respective states and focused on building the capacity of officials such that (1) focus is shifted from IEC to BCC; (2) initiatives or communication campaigns for demand generation are evidence driven and scientifically designed to support measurability/ monitoring; (3) increased attention is laid on standardization of communication initiatives while being cognizant of the contextual norms; and (4) building evidence and sharing knowledge on the approach. 

UNICEF followed two approaches of setting up SBCC Cells: 

1. Strengthening the existing IEC Bureau within state machinery (e.g., Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Gujarat, Maharashtra, Uttar Pradesh, West Bengal, Assam, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh). This was done through the provision of extensive technical support in SBC knowledge and skills using several interventions and through the placement of skilled SBC professionals in the existing IEC unit structures. 

2. Setting up SBCC Cells in state institutions that are best suited for setting up centers for communication e.g., Communication Resource Unit in Andhra Pradesh and Centre of Excellence in Odisha. 

Most Cells were housed within the Directorate of Health Services; herein the key focus of the Cells was on health programmes including RI and MCH. SBCC Cells hosted by allied departments such as ICDS have focused on wider health or social determinants including WASH, child protection, child marriage, education, etc. 

## 4.2. Understanding of the SBCC cells in the evaluation from secondary literature review 

In India’s SBCC models, communication has been instrumental in advancing health-related outcomes such as RMNCH+A, and Routine Immunization (RI) coverage, greater awareness of women and maternal child health, including nutrition, and vaccination, and WASH. States such as Assam, Odisha, and Madhya Pradesh have pioneered the establishment and operationalizing the cells. Whereas states like Uttar Pradesh, Odisha, West Bengal, as also Madhya Pradesh aimed at institutionalizing the SBCC Cells at the district level for greater mobilization of resources. States such as Telangana has mobilized efforts beyond the given needs of RMNCH+A and RI such as ODF and RCCE and established the Communication Resource Unit (CRU). In view of this, the organizational structure of the cell in each state differ to a large extent as elucidated below *[38] 

The SBCC cell in Assam is housed under the health department and is institutionalized at the state level.  The state has two SBCC cells at the state level both by National Health Mission (NHM) and  the Directorate of Health Services, Family Welfare (DHSFW), with some common SBCC activities undertaken by both NHM and DHSFW such as polio, etc. Assam was one among the first states to have established a BCC cell in 2007 brought under NRHM for binging together all health communications at on place. The second SBCC cell under the DHSFW focuses on communication related to Routine Immunization (RI) to address and eliminate the surrounding misconceptions that have so far constrained the state from attaining full immunization coverage. The nodal officers of the cell are DG DHS(FW), NHM.  The communication strategies were revised in 2014 to address issues around demand generation for health service utilization to meet the NHM objectives using a more comprehensive 

> 38 Source: Presentations by state SBCC cells SBCC Reports Case State study 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

16 

approach to communication by utilizing all feasible modes of media to reach communities across all socioeconomic profiles. 

In Odisha, the State Institute of Health and Family Welfare has been a nodal organization for executing SBCC activities at the state and district level after its establishment in 1994. SIHFW was formulated because a gap was identified along the lines of - building capacities of managers at the state and the district level and frontline workers. To this end, a Centre of Excellence was set up inside the SIHFW. The Departments of Health and Family Welfare, Women and Child Development, Rural Development, OPEPA and OSACS were collaborators in this effort. It was envisaged that this would enhance technical understanding of the staff and would assist formulation of communication strategy and its effective implementation. The COE was further envisioned to design and develop quality material on behavior change communication, forge partnerships with recognized institutions to deliver capsule training on BCC, to provide research support on communication programs of the state and to provide technical support in procuring services related to communication material and management. The CoE has three major components - (a) a program management unit, (b) a material and design management unit and (c) a finance management unit. Integrated BCC cells are present in each of the 31 districts of Odisha. 

Uttar Pradesh was among the first few states to establish state level SBCC cell, in 2014. The cell was introduced into the public health system of UP with the support from UNICEF and was designed to act as a state-level body that would guide the district communication cadre to undertake BCC activities. The nodal officer for the cell is CMOs. UNICEF works mainly in five priority districts – Balarampur, Mirzapur, Shravasti, Jaunpur, and Sonbhadra. Balarampur and Mirzapur are two districts where the SBCC cells have been set up and supported by UNICEF. Over the years, with the support of Technical Support Group for Behavior Change Communication (TSG-BCC) under the Health Partner’s Forum (HPF) involving representatives from the GoUP, NHM, UNICEF, Save the Children, MAMTA, TSU, Plan India, BBC Media, and other development partners is responsible for developing a comprehensive strategy/plan and its implementation. In the past few years, the state has witnessed several efforts focused towards reducing the overall IMR, MMR and increasing Institutional Deliveries, Immunization, Early Breast Feeding, MR campaign, COVID-19 (prevention and vaccination) etc. 

The state of Madhya Pradesh had functional IEC bureau under Department of Health and Family Welfare. The SBCC state plan was incorporated as a division in the state IEC cell. The SBCC Cell focuses on communications related to immunization, nutrition, and healthcare. Technical assistance for the same is given by the SBCC cell and implementation is undertaken by IEC. The nodal person for cell at state level are Director (IAS officer), Deputy Director (medical officer), Media Education and Information Officer (MEIO) and SBCC coordinator. The thematic areas of the cell include addressing the existing inequality in health-related challenges, women’s nutrition, and vaccination using mediums like mass media, social media, print media, folk music using local artists have been used as tools of communication for successful IEC/SBCC campaigns. The SBCC Cells are also present at the district level and fairly actively engaging with the government, development partners and communities on behavior change related to nutrition, MCH, etc.  District level SBCC cells are present in Guna and Shivpuri districts in Madhya Pradesh. 

The SBCC cell in Telangana - Communication Resource Unit (CRU was established by National Institute for Rural Development (NIRD) and Panchayat Raj (PR) in collaboration with UNICEF to address gaps in government departments’ capacities to design and implement BCC strategies. Telangana has opted to establish only one dedicated SBCC cell at the state level and restrained from developing district level cells in the state. It aims to provide an institutional mechanism for strategic communication support to various government departments and national flagship programmes. It caters to strategic communication needs through consultations, technical support and capacity building. The nodal officers for CRU include Professor and Head and 2 SBCC Coordinators. Thematic areas of CRU are tribal development, women and child development, education, rural wash, Panchayati raj and rural development and health. 

The West Bengal SBCC cell has been institutionalized under Department of Women and Child Development (DWCD). The nodal officers include secretary in consultation with MIC, District Magistrate, Additional District Magistrate, Deputy Magistrate, District Nodal Officer. Cell emphasizes on participatory planning for improving utilization of IEC/SBCC budgeting thematic areas of sanitation, Health, child marriage, nutrition, education and violence against children. The cell gathers evidences and focuses on increasing institutional delivery and increase RI in hard-to-reach areas, achieved ODF, increase school attendance, engaging faith leaders to prevent child marriage. SBCC cells are present in districts Purulia, 24 South Parganas, Murshidabad, Dakshin Dinajpur and Malda, where they are institutionalized within respective District Magistrate’s office. 

## **Challenges in SBCC Strategy Implementation** 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

17 

Social and Behavioural Change Communication (SBCC) is a key aspect of awareness generation and creating demand for the available health and welfare services. UNICEF is working actively along with state government towards building capacities of Frontline Health Workers (FHW) to deliver SBCC programs in rural areas by to the needs of vulnerable communities. However, implementation of SBCC components have seen various challenges over the years. 

SBCC programs require wide scale participation by stakeholders across different sectors like various government departments, development partners, civil society, local governance bodies, media and entertainment.  However, it is seen that the engagement of all relevant stakeholders in the development of SBCC strategy has been non uniform and inadequate across states which acts as a hindrance to convergent action. Literature suggests that SBCC strategies can make desired behavior change when resources across various sectors are successfully leveraged[39] . 

A situation analysis of behavior change communication (BCC) and health promotion activities within the existing national health programs suggests that, reported scarcity of funds and human resources to support appropriate BCC and health promotion activities at the community and health center level. In addition, fund allocation and financial planning is still heavily focused on printing of IEC material[40] . Similarly, a study highlights physical/material gaps, administrative issues and training requirements of the staff in addition to resource challenges[41] . Another suggests that availability of IEC materials at ground level was a challenge despite ample availability of the material due to local logistic issues[42] 

Additionally, there is a lack of structured reporting formats to capture the programme activities leading to challenges in documentation. Similarly, the monitoring and evaluation of the interventions was limited on account of lack of trained staff and resources to conduct and manage M&E[43] . 

Given this discourse, following are five critical factors considered during the evaluation: 

- 1) Engagement of all relevant departments in the development of SBCC strategy 

- 2) Level of awareness of SBCC strategy across relevant departments and officials 

- 3) Resource procurement, management and utilization 

- 4) Availability of funds, rationale of allocation and budget utilization 

## 4.3. Objective and scope of the evaluation 

The purpose of the evaluation is to understand the SBCC Cells’ performance towards maximizing utilization of allocated resources and augmenting the results of the health systems strengthening initiatives and other cross sectoral themes of social and health determinants at the state and district level. The evaluation is intended to further provide critical programmatic insights into what has worked and what has not worked and why. The findings will further strengthen behavior change initiatives aimed directly at maximizing utilization of public services. This evaluation is intended to act as a guiding document for UNICEF to decide its future engagement and investment in the SBCC program. 

The programme will be evaluated for the period 2017-2021. The cells will be evaluated on the five parameters of the HSS framework namely, leadership and governance, workforce, service delivery, finance, and information, against five OECD DAC evaluation criteria of relevance, efficiency, effectiveness, coherence, and sustainability. Gender and equity lens will be cross cutting in reviewing the Cells’ performance and milestones on the aforementioned evaluation criteria. 

The geographical scope of the evaluation is across six states namely, Madhya Pradesh, Uttar Pradesh, West Bengal, Assam, Telangana, and Odisha. The objective of the evaluation is to understand the ‘why’ and the ‘how’ of the success or failure of the initiative and provide evidence-based recommendations on making the functioning

## 5.1. Conceptual framework: Theory of change 

Based on document review, consultation with UNICEF ICO and UNICEF SBC specialists across the states under focus for this study, a retrospective Theory of Change (ToC) has been developed for the SBCC Cells. The activities of the Cell are categorised under the five pillars of Health System Strengthening including: (1) Leadership and governance; (2) Workforce; (3) Service Delivery; (4) Finance; and (5) Information. The overall intent of the SBCC Cell emerged to provide support for improvement of demand generation and accelerate uptake of government schemes that can positively impact a child’s wellbeing through enhanced and equitable access to government sponsored services/schemes for health, nutrition, education, water, sanitation, and protection. Accordingly, the outcomes for this model are: (1) Sustainable government systems created for SBCC programming; (2) Improved service delivery* and outreach of government programmes; (3) Improved uptake and beneficiary participation towards government sponsored programmes/schemes; and (4) Positive policy environment created for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives. 

To attain this intent, the **pathways of change** are designed as following: 

- If the leadership is sensitised on the need for behavioural change, then increased number of programmes will adopt SBCC at the design and result stage 

- If SBCC Cell is institutionalised under the government set-up then SBCC-based programming will be more sustainable, accountable, and transparent 

- If SBCC relevant content is integrated in programme planning and capacity development, then beneficiary identification, targeting, coverage and outreach will improve 

- If IPC capability of functionaries is strengthened, then their convincing power and quality of service delivery will improve 

- If the desired behaviour is established, then communities will actively participate in monitoring service delivery and thereby demand for improved service quality 

**Overarching assumptions:** Availability of a nodal govt department/institutional set up to host the SBCC Cell; adequate resource mobilization by nodal department/institution in supporting competent HR and financial budgets; availability of a network of community mobilizers to support awareness and demand for services. 

## **Risks:** 

- Given the vast socio-cultural diversity, inability to identify behavioural triggers based on social norms and cultural practices 

- If housed within a Directorate, difficulty in convergence and resource pooling 

- If not housed under a Directorate, difficulty in getting participation from functionaries and officers (under other line departments) at the district and community level 

- Restricted budget for mix-media integration 

- Inhibition of shifting from IEC based approach 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

21 

- Limited digital literacy amongst service providers and community representatives to adopt/harness technology enabled communication channels (particularly in post COVID era) 

In collating the information, it was noted that the SBCC Cells under review have distinctive characteristics, which although similar, are not comparable. According to the nature of institution and the thematic area of focus, the states were clubbed under three categories (1) SBCC Cells housed under the health department and based at the state level – Assam; (2) SBCC Cells housed under the health department, based at state and district level - Odisha, Madhya Pradesh, Uttar Pradesh; and (3) SBCC Cells housed outside the Health Department: West Bengal (with district level cells), Telangana (without district level cells). Accordingly, the team had developed (1) an overarching ToC capturing the broad intent and core areas of focus or change; and (2) three supporting ToCs as per the categorization of the states. This section provides an overarching ToC which will be finalized in the next phase (data collection) in detailed consultation with the states and ICO. Figure 2 below highlights the contents of the overarching ToC developed.

## 5.2. Evaluation design 

The evaluation focused on key building blocks in the structure, design, and operation of SBCC Cells. The core themes included nature of workforce, mechanism of service delivery, financing, leadership and design and execution of governance and information and knowledge management. Along with the OECD DAC evaluation criteria, the evaluation also included the domain of gender and social equity. In the detailed evaluation framework (refer to Annexure 1), we have mapped indicators with the KEQs as per all the overall evaluation criteria.

**Figure 3: Representative image of evaluation sites** 

## 5.3. Sampling design 

## i. Selection of states 

The assessment was planned across **six states** in India across the country’s regions: Assam, West Bengal, Odisha, Jharkhand, Telangana and Madhya Pradesh. The states were selected purposively considering UNICEF’s categorization of high burden states (Madhya Pradesh), tribal (Odisha, North-East), good governance (Telangana). Geographical variance in the eastern region (North-East (Assam), East (Odisha, West Bengal), Central (Madhya Pradesh and Jharkhand) and South (Telangana) was ensured, providing a more representative picture of the program. However, Jharkhand, a sample state for the evaluation, was later replaced with Uttar Pradesh. This change was made considering that the SBCC Cell in Jharkhand has been handed over to the government. prior to the evaluation period. Hence, the evaluation of this cell would not enable assessment of UNICEF’s role in supporting the cell during the evaluation period. 

Evaluation in the states of Odisha, Madhya Pradesh, West Bengal, and Uttar Pradesh was done at two levels as there are two levels of SBCC Cells, at the state and district level. In the remaining two states of, Assam and Telangana, the SBCC Cells only function at the state level, hence the evaluation was carried out at the state level. 

**==> picture [227 x 270] intentionally omitted <==**

## ii. Selection of districts/blocks 

We understand that marginalized communities including scheduled castes, scheduled tribes, religious minorities, and persons with disabilities have been historically excluded and may be under-represented/covered/reported across development programmes intervention focus and results. While there have been efforts towards taking into consideration the need for greater intervention focus, and integration of such stakeholders within active planning and implementation of SBCC and related programming, there are further opportunities no ted in this regard.[45] In this context, a stepwise methodology was used to ensure diversity of population in the selection of districts in the sample states. 

**Step 1:** Within diversity of population, district level values of percentage of scheduled caste population, percentage of scheduled tribe population, percentage of population of religious minorities and total district level population of Persons with Disability was mapped across the sample states. The Census 2011[46] data was considered for the above-mentioned indicators. 

**Step 2:** Two districts per sample state were selected based on values mapped across indicators mentioned above, elaborating the overall diversity of population[47] . One district with a lower than state average value across the selected indicators and another with a higher than state average value across the selected indicators per state was selected. Further, distance from state capital was also considered in the process of district selection.

## 5.4. Data collection methods 

The data was collected through qualitative interview guides/ FGD guide. Pilot testing of both the qualitative and FGD guides was conducted to assess the feasibility of data collection tools in Mandar district of Jharkhand in September- October 2022. After the successful completion of the pilot testing, a team consultation was arranged, and the tools were revised based on the observations from the pilot. The revisions included incorporating new areas of inquiry which emerged during the discussion, and feedback received on the questions framed during the pilot testing. 

The tools were administered by a trained research team, oriented, and trained on the study methodology and technical components of interview/ FGD guides. The study team who conducted the interviews were public health professionals with prior experience of conducting such studies. On field training of the team for conducting the interviews was also carried out. Safety protocols were followed during the data collection to counter the spread of COVID-19 and protect all the individuals associated. 

To ensure the quality of collected data, certain identified aspects were verified through observations such as availability of IEC material (Flyers, posters, media clips on social media), display of IEC material at the SBCC cell, and health centers etc. And those related to routine practices were assessed through observation and record reviews. Similarly, the team corroborated aspects on capacity building by SBCC cells by observation of training like ToT for master resource person and other capacity building sessions. 

The questionnaire was administered in Hindi/ English based on respondents’ preference. FGD guide was administered in local languages taking the help of translators. Translators were provided with an orientation on the study methodology and FGD guide beforehand. The team of researchers was trained to administer the questionnaire in the respective languages. The qualitative interviews were audio-recorded after getting the oral consent of the respondents. In case the consent for audio recordings was not obtained, manual notes were taken for the same.  On average, the interviews took 30 minutes each. The interview was stopped when information saturation was reached i.e., no new information was emerging from the interaction. Data collection in each state took five days of time, by a team of 2-3 researchers. Data was collected during November 2022 to January 2023. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

26 

## 5.5. Analytical approaches 

## **Desk review and secondary data analysis** 

A large volume and a comprehensive number of documents were reviewed in this evaluation including the program documents shared by the state SBCC cells including state case studies, power point presentations and communication material and reports publications by UNICEF including C4D progress reports. Detailed list of 

The secondary data was subjected to content analysis to get insights on aspects such as structure of SBCC cells, its functional and thematic areas and key challenges in functioning. 

## **Qualitative interviews and focused group discussions** 

The evaluation team considered the design of the programme, its organic evolution, its’ state-specific context, variations, and core areas of focus while developing the data analysis approach. 

Given that this study is primarily qualitative in nature, the data was analysed as follows: 

**Interviews and Focus groups discussion** : Coding of the transcriptions was conducted using reflexive thematic analysis. Themes were mapped to various components of the Organization for Economic Co-operation, and Development Assistance Committee (OECD-DAC) evaluation criteria of relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, coherence, and sustainability.  Coding framework was shared with the larger study team to form the consensus, and accordingly finalized. The study findings have used illustrative quotations in English where required. Coding and analysis of qualitative data was carried out using Microsoft Excel. 

**Field level Observation:** The data for this is drawn from the inputs gathered across the parameters noted in the observation checklist. This information was added under relevant sections of state wise findings 

Steps followed in analysis of qualitative data- 

**Step 1: Transcription of the recorded interviews** /discussions into English were done manually. Particular care was taken during the transcription so that there is no loss of information during the process. To ensure quality of transcriptions, researchers and supervisors undertook random checks by listening to audio recordings. 

**Step 2: Development of coding framework and coding of qualitative data:** Coding framework was developed as per the KEQs and SEQs to categorize responses using the information areas covered in the discussion guides. However, this list was augmented based on other codes that emerged from the transcripts during the actual coding process. After carefully reading the transcriptions, information from transcriptions was put into the relevant code 

Triangulation of qualitative interview data and other secondary information was conducted to synthesize the evaluation findings and draw conclusions.

## 5.6. Limitations of the study 

- The use of quantitative and numerical data points pertaining to health, behavioral and social indicators is limited within the scope of this evaluation. A link of causality and/or attributability cannot be formed between said datapoints and findings of the study. The scope of the study is limited to understanding contribution of SBCC cell in health system strengthening. 

- We could not cover the target sample as we could not conduct few stakeholder consultations across state, district and block levels owing to unavailability of relevant stakeholders or prior commitments to other engagements or unresponsiveness to communications shared requesting for consultations. However, the response rate for the study was approximately 90 percent, which is acceptable in social science research. 

- There was limited awareness of SBCC related activities among both trainers, trainees of SBCC and community members across a few sites. Further, the recently transferred recruitments had limited institutional memory on the evolution of the SBCC Cell in the state. Limited relevant insights were received on the nature of work done and challenges faced in doing so and evolution of SBCC cell through the interactions conducted with the above stakeholder groups. 

- There is a lack of any prior in-depth, comprehensive baseline studies that could offer insights into all dimensions of SBCC programming, that could further be used or referred to during the evaluation. Hence, it was observed that the gaps in covering and strengthening SBCC programmes in certain areas in each state was largely attributed to the absence of on-ground research and profiling based on demographic and socioeconomic status of the populations in the respective settlements. 

- There was lack of institutional memory within the government related to SBCC cell including relevant documentation which might have resulted in recall bias. The evaluation team tried to include appropriate probes in the questionnaire to aid respondents’ recall of activities. The team obtained relevant documents from the state SBCC team to fulfil the objectives of the evaluation. 

- As there is no state level SBCC Cell in Uttar Pradesh, the stakeholders mapped at the state level were from different IEC cells across line departments with limited technical context and awareness of SBCC related activities. As a result, the input received from such consultations highlighted limited relevant insights on the same. 

- In Assam, West Karbi, Anglong was one of the proposed evaluation districts initially. However, we had  to change the district to Morigaon due to conflict in the district. 

- The good practices that we have documented in the report have been compiled by information received from the respondents and we have not validated the same through field observations. 

## 5.7. Ethics and UNEG Standards 

Data collection team comprised of PwC staff including senior consultant and manager. All members of the team completed a foundational ethics training specifically UNICEF’s course ‘Introduction to Ethics in Evidence Generation’ on Agora before conducting the evaluation. 

The study team abided by 2021 UNICEF procedure on ethics and Evaluation and UNEG norms and standards for Evaluation. The team involved in primary data collection were oriented on protocols for ensuring compliance to ethics, gender sensitivity and equity principles in data collection and management. The data collection methods employed for the evaluation did not include any interaction with children or adolescents. 

All stakeholders and evaluation participants identified for consultations and data collection, were duly informed on the study, its intent, how the input received is proposed to be used and limits of confidentiality. Mandatory oral consent was obtained from all the respondents before the interview / discussion, and any queries, concerns, or questions were addressed prior to the interview / discussion. 

All respondents were informed about the voluntary nature of participation in the study and if they wish to withdraw after the consent, then they are free to do so. Each project team member engaged in the ensured adherence to non-disclosure of any data received or accessed in course of the evaluation. 

The study team undertook measures to maintain the confidentiality of the respondents. During the process of synthesizing of findings personal identifiers were removed from the database and unique identification codes (UID) were used while analyzing the data. All interview data including audio files of recordings and transcriptions 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

28 

were handled as per the research protocols and the project manager ensured that access to data was restricted and shared on a need-to-know and use basis. 

Protocols for interactions and data collection including on-field conduct, particularly with women, and other vulnerable groups, were defined. Adherence to "do no harm" principle, respect for dignity and diversity, compliance with codes was ensured for the same. Further, team was trained and periodically oriented on the protocols. To maintain the quality of evaluation we ensured following points. 

- Clarity of roles and reporting lines were well defined. Each person had specific responsibilities and clear job descriptions. Their outputs and deliverables directly contributed to the project’s overall impact. 

- Each team member had clear objectives at the start of each project year in discussion with the project leadership team. There was a protocol to update the TL (Team Leader) and project manager daily o n field interactions and proceedings. The performance of the core team was assessed on a monthly basis. Training and mentoring were ensured if any member faced challenge in delivering quality results. 

- The TL and the subject matter experts were engaged at state level interactions. Spot quality control checks were in place to address grievance if any during the evaluation on field.
