## 6. Findings 

The SBCC Cell Evaluation covered six states namely, Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Assam, West Bengal, Odisha, and Telangana. The findings have been triangulated from various sources of data such as secondary literature review, UNICEF state team presentations and primary data collection across all six states. 

Each state’s Cell structure can be categorised broadly as those which are housed within the health department, those which are outside of the purview of the health department, those which are with state level institutional setup and those with the state as well as district level institutional setup. 

|**Category**|**State**|
|---|---|
|**Category 1**: SBCC cells housed under the health<br>dept. and based at the state level|Assam|
|**Category 2**: SBCC cells housed under the health<br>department, based at state and district level|Uttar Pradesh, Odisha and Madhya Pradesh|
|**Category 3**: SBCC cells housed outside the<br>Health Department|West Bengal (with district cells) and Telangana<br>(no district cells)|



The findings are presented across these categories and have been mapped against the key evaluation questions across the five OECD-DAC criteria of Relevance, Effectiveness, Efficiency, Sustainability and Coherence. The evaluation includes an overall cross-cutting theme of gender and equity embedded within the adopted methodology and are presented in the findings accordingly. To establish a holistic understanding about the evaluation criteria, each of these have been elaborated below: 

## 6.1. Relevance 

Relevance as an evaluation parameter aims to analyse the extent to which the intervention objectives and design respond to beneficiaries, global, country, and partner/institution needs, policies, and priorities, and continue to do so if circumstances change. To evaluate the level of relevance for the SBCC program, state level officials, UNICEF consultants (loaned staff) and district level officials of SBCC cells were asked to comment upon the program’s alignment to national and state level flagship programs, usage of established systems of delivery and the cells’ response to community needs. Similarly, to assess the adaptability of SBCC cells to COVID 19 response by assessing modified approach to SBCC activities during the pandemic. 

KEQ 1: How relevant and appropriate are the SBCC Cells to the flagship programs, and priorities of the national and state governments? 

## **Category 1** 

There is a relevance of the SBCC Cells to the flagship programs particularly as they are under the National Health Mission (NHM) and with the Directorate of Health Services, Family Welfare (DHSFW). The integrated SBCC activities are aimed at addressing health topics and behaviors for improved health and development outcomes. Mission Indradhanush has been a major action-oriented programme of the Cell to strengthen immunization coverage in priority districts which have less than 50 per cent unvaccinated or partially vaccinated children. Mission Tejaswee was aimed at reducing maternal, neonatal and child mortality. Over the years, special immunization drives have targeted children and expectant mothers to increase the immunization coverage to 90 per cent. Although Assam made considerable improvements in Routine Immunization, currently, the Cell’s focus is on urban immunization, as it was found that immunization coverage is lower in urban areas as compared to rural areas. The relevance of RI has helped to have wider reach of demand generation for RI activities. Largely, the Call to Action for Child Survival and Development initiative by the Govt. of India was concurrent with the Reproductive, Maternal, New-born Child Health +Adolescent (RMNCH+A) Strategy for addressing child survival and reduction of Infant Mortality Rate (IMR), and Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR). SBCC communication comprised mass media spots, open book wall painting, folk media, community theatre, rallies by school children, dissemination of IPC (ICDS and health service providers, and short videos and ASHA radio programs on 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

30 

Immunization which were some of the key strategies. During Covid-19, the importance of hygiene and sanitation were reiterated through key messages on handwashing, social distancing, etc. District task force meetings were organized for SBCC coordinators across all 33 districts of Assam. The BCC activities have been under the Child Health or Maternal Health are included in their respective sections. The SBCC cell under NHM receive financial support through the State’s PIP, as the NHM PIP is the main source of funding. IEC-related planning has been the aggregate of the budget of IEC under the umbrella of the NHM at state level. 

## **Category 2** 

SBCC cells in category two are housed under the health department and hence work well within the ambit of flagship programmes of this department. The cells act as a facilitator of communication activities for various health interventions and programs. For example, in UP the cell contributes to programs such as Mission Indra Dhanush, Dastak Sanchari and Measles and rubella awareness; in Odisha the cell contributes to programs for routine immunization and RMNCHA+; and in Madhya Pradesh the cell has been able to contribute to programs such as Beti Bachao Beti Padhao. The cells aid in developing strategic plans and materials for integrating SBCC programming in flagship health programs of the state and national level. For example, in UP to increase vaccine coverage under Mission Indra Dhanush, the cell was able to recognise the importance of interpersonal communication (IPC) to increase a trust building process between the community members and frontline health workers. The cell was then able to train the frontline health workers in the skills of IPC which aided the said workers in spreading awareness about the importance of vaccination and reduce vaccine hesitancy. 

Further, since the cells monetary allocation comes from the respective flagship programs, their capacity to function and develop strategic plans is restricted to the aspects of said programs. 

## **Category 3** 

In the case of category 3, the cells are housed outside of the health department under NIRDPR (CRU) ad WCD (West Bengal cell). Hence, the cells’ functioning does not align with core health flagship programs such as Mission Indra Dhanush; rather the cells’ functioning aligns with supporting health programs related to nutrition, WASH and sanitation. For example, CRU in Telangana has contributed to flagship programs such as SBM, JJM and Poshan Abhiyan by developing communication strategies which focus on integrating SBCC into the planning and programming stages of these programs, providing training to master trainers who are able to further disseminate their learnings at the field level and contextualization of SBCC and IEC material by translating to local languages and changing of social examples and imagery to those which are locally relevant. 

For the cell in West Bengal, the expected area of focus is ICDS for Nutrition and the four largest schemes viz., Kanyasree, Rupasree, Lakshmir Bhandar and Social Welfare schemes such as old age and widow pension. The cell contributes to these programs by developing differentiated district wise communications plans based on community needs. The expected role of the cell is that of a convergence agency connecting the dots required for communications around the focus areas of WCD such as nutrition. 

**==> picture [476 x 63] intentionally omitted <==**

KEQ 2: How relevant, appropriate and aligned is the design of the SBCC Cell to the State’s established systems and institutions of service delivery? 

## **Category 1** 

The Health Communication in Assam is implemented by the District Extension Media Officer (DEMO), (now the state Cold Chain officer under NHM. The other members include DHSFW at the district level, Block Community Mobilizer (BCM), NHM and Health Educator (HE)/Block Extension Educator, DHSFW at the blocks. ASHA and ASHA Supervisors are the main frontline cadre involved in informing communities on available health services, disseminating health messages and assisting in utilization of health services. In addition to immunization, service delivery at the state level have comprised various innovations towards reduction of mortality, cleft-lip, and distribution of iron tablets in school, etc such as through the Rural Health Practitioners (RHP). Furthermore, the state is looking at launching the Boat Ambulance and Clinic concept (a good practice from Bangladesh) as was shared by an SBCC officer participant, the Mobile Medical Unit (MMU), ASHA Radio Program, Sarathi-104 (Health Information Helpline & Complaint Redressal system), etc. However, on ground it was found that accessibility of these in remote and hard-to-reach areas such as the _Chars_ – areas or lands which are surrounded by water, 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

31 

were difficult, and still a major concern. The gaps in Reproductive Maternal Newborn Child and Adolescent Heath (RMNCH+A), have been addressed and reduced although services and knowledge and awareness related to more fundamental issues of child/early marriage, teen pregnancy, and school dropout, need to be addressed and tackled for integration of social behavior change, and health concerns related to adolescents and women. This is particularly true of the two districts of Dhubri and Morigaon in the evaluation study where it was found that the aforementioned practices are highly prevalent in LODOR groups, largely in the economically vulnerable and hardto-each areas. These two districts are also the ones which have seen the least intersectoral convergence among the 33 districts during Covid-19. The cells in this category occasionally converge fundamentally in RI work, polio and vaccination, although collaborations with other departments are not yet strengthened, specifically to tackle issues of early marriage and adolescent SRH which need more attention and convergence. 

## **Category 2** 

The cells in category two are highly aligned to the states’ established systems and institutions of service delivery. Since the cells are housed under the health department, they are able to leverage and make use of established systems such as the differentiated cadres of frontline health workers. Further, the cells do not have a dedicated communications cadre and hence comprise of members who are government employees in the positions of health officials, hence leveraging the state’s established human resource cadre within the health system. Even the cells’ activities are planned in accordance with the established programming and timelines of the health department. Hence, the cells’ functioning is not only aligned but can be said to be dependent on the states’ established systems and institutions of service delivery. 

Further, the cells in this category have positive convergence and collaborations with other relevant departments such as their respective DWCD. This allows for even greater access to the states’ established systems and institutions of service delivery. For example, the cells in this category are able to leverage the Anganwadi system too under the DWCD, in addition to the established systems of the health department. 

## **Category 3** 

The cells in category three have access to the state’s established systems and institutions of service delivery of the respective department they are housed under. For eg, CRU has access to leverage the PRI cadre as it is housed under NIRDPR and the cell in West Bengal has access to the Angwadi system as it is house under DWCD. Further, the cells do not have a dedicated communications cadre and hence comprise of members who are government employees in the positions of ICDS and PRI officials, hence leveraging the state’s established human resource cadre within the state’s established systems. 

For the cell in West Bengal, even the cells’ activities are planned in accordance with the established programming and timelines of the DWCD. 

CRU has an added advantage of being housed under a national institute which gives it access to the institutes’ established systems and institutions of service delivery (SIRDs) at the state level too. 

KEQ 3: To what extent does the SBCC Cell respond to the needs of the community members and community representatives? 

## **Category 1** 

One of the major activities of the Cell’s engagement in responding to the needs of communities and representatives has been in the RMNCH+A coverage, and to increase awareness amongst the mothers and communities about the need of ANC and post-partum health. Although, the key focus and approach has been on the (six) High Priority Districts, there is an overall gap in, and the need to cover all vulnerable and hard -to-reach areas across the state’s 33 districts. It was noted that communication gaps exist in tea garden areas of indigenous populations, and char areas which do not yet have a flow of transport facilities. It was also found that there is a limited capacity of FLHWs to tackle issues of ineffectual communication. Most FLWS in the workforce is currently aging and ae close to retirement, while there is concurrently, a shortage of FLHWs due to a lack of recruitment. The FLHWs need continued and advanced training in improving their interpersonal skills to engage better with communities. A direct impact of this scenario has led to the impact of demand for service utilization. There is a lack of detailed, lucid explanation of locally relevant SBCC material particularly for indigenous populations speaking dialects. Thus, there is a need to create a communication strategy with appropriate and relevant messages for specific populations in specific areas. During Covid-19, the SBCC officers have themselves travelled to sensitive districts for addressing vaccine hesitancy of communities, by self -demonstrating the criticality of vaccine on themselves. Further, SBCC Cells have reached out to religious leaders and other Faith Based Organizations through an orientation, such that the importance of covid-19 vaccination, and immunization, 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

32 

and early marriage prevention can be addressed. However, not much has been addressed as wider SBCC activities need to be undertaken with coordinated efforts at the district and block levels which need intervention of SBCC Cells’ officers. Although there is one SBCC coordinator in each district, there is a need for capacity building. At present, IEC materials are the usual brochures and pamphlets which the FLWs and community influencers use as guiding sources for information. The overarching health issues related to mothers and expectant mothers In the last few years have increased mainly with attention to vaccine, RI, measles, etc. However, system strengthening through sufficient budgeting, M&E etc for state-level plans for RI across 33 districts in the state are necessary. For example, IMR/MMR, child marriage, etc are social concerns that are not yet addressed with any preventive measures. Reportedly, there is a case of child (under-age) marriage at 32 percent in Assam. Departments can play a role in better integration and convergence, but there is a limitation and challenge on account of no convening powers. Jingles, banners are not relevant and sufficient as awareness needs to be generated through actual on-groundwork. FLWs need advanced training to be able to be more communicable with communities. Conclusively, the targeted health-related activities and communication have been carried out within the usual and prescribed tasks of the SBCC programmes, but crucial and urgent social concerns of women and adolescent SRH, teen pregnancy, safeguarding rights of girls (early marriage), and school dropout need urgent responsive and preventive measures need to be set to be able to address community needs and generate awareness. 

**==> picture [476 x 54] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Category 2** 

Since the cells function not only at the state level but also at a district level, they are in regular communication with community members and representatives such as frontline health workers. They are able to provide regular guidance and oversight of dissemination of health messaging at the community level. Though there is a major limitation in the cells ability to respond to the needs of community members and representatives due to resource and budget constraints. It was found that the cells’ approach is top down as they function only on the mandate of the state government and have no flexibility to include findings from formal and informal needs assessments done at the community level. Since the cells are provided with monetary allocation through the budget for each respective program, they are given detailed instructions on how much to spend and are restricted to spend the fund only on items given on a state mandated list of IEC and SBCC materials. 

**==> picture [476 x 55] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Category 3** 

CRU does not have a mandate to work at the community or implementation level. Hence, it does not directly contribute to responding to the needs of community members and representatives. Though it works closely with allied departments like WCD which have grassroot level presence and utilizes secondary data to analyse and understand the needs of community members while designing the SBCC programme for various programs under different departments. 

Since the cells in West Bengal functions at a district level, they are in regular communication with community members and representatives such as frontline health workers. They are able to provide regular guidance and oversight of dissemination of health messaging at the community level. Further, the cells are able to contribute to responding to the needs of the community members by developing customised strategies for communications focused on local contexts and the needs of community members and representatives. 

KEQ 4: How adaptable is the SBCC Cell to evolving concerns/areas of focus/evidence for realignment (including COVID) 

## **Category 1** 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

33 

During Covid-19, the SBCC cells were active in rolling out vaccines, and most people in the cells were directly or indirectly involved in vaccine roll out programmes. As part of the Covid-19 communication activities, state cells covered populations under the 33 districts, wherein district coordinators visited sessions which were conducted, and gathered information through mobile phones. The district coordinators covered and conducted these interactive sessions to address logistical issues of vaccine accessibility of diverse populations groups. It was noted that the state was the first towards attaining effective vaccine management (EVM). However, as was found that at the state and district level, there was no direct Covid-management of medical activities of doctors; rather it was largely focused on administrative tasks. In terms of adaptability, post-Covid, social media has not had a substantial role, even though it is a pressing need with comprehensive awareness communication material for MR issues, and additionally, there is no information generation or awareness/coverage for RI through social media. Furthermore, no training has been conducted in the last 2 years for ASHA/Aanganwadi workers, while No BRIDGE training has been conducted post virtual training that was conducted during Covid. The lack of training of FLHWs has been a barrier in improving and advancing issues on communication and attitudes related to health both from a micro-level and macro level perspectives. 

## **Category 2** 

As the cells are housed under the health department, and with COVID-19 being a public health issue, the cells were able to contribute to the COVID response for their respective states. The cells were able to conduct online training for frontline health workers when social distancing was recommended during the pandemic. Further, the cells were able to train frontline health workers to use novel means of communication such as telephone and WhatsApp to deliver health messaging to community members while maintaining physical distance. 

The cells were also able to act as a repository for online IEC and SBCC material which was sent out to the frontline health workers and health officials to be spread amongst community members. The cells have been able to adapt to other evolving concerns too such as being introduced to an increased focus on ODF practices. 

## **Category 3** 

The cells in this category reported to have flexibility to be able to adapt to evolving concerns and areas of focus. For example, CRU was able to contribute to designing and providing technical assistance in developing Covid19 response not just for the states in serves (namely Andhra Pradesh, Telangana and Karnataka) but also for other states such as Sikkim. Largely, at the state level, the cells are able to adapt national level IEC material into regionally contextual material includes translation into local languages and changing of imagery. For West Bengal, at the district level, citizen-centric steps are being taken to have a wider reach and needs of the target beneficiaries. The districts level cells work on issues of institutional delivery, child marriage, adolescent health, malnutrition, menstrual hygiene etc. The cells have been very active during COVID 19 and mobilization during COVID vaccination. The district level cells were able to act as a convergence point for BMOHs and frontline health workers during the pandemic hi. SBCC cell aligns with the needs arising from time to time. 

## **Conclusion** 

The cells in all three categories are gradually working towards the pathways of change indicated for the first and fourth longer term outcomes* in the theory of change namely, institutionalization of SBCC programming through sustainable systemic reforms, and positive policy environment created for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives. The outputs pertaining to relevance as an evaluation indicator are leadership and officials being trained on SBCC programming reflects the shift from IEC to SBCC and joint programmes being planned and organised with allied sectors and meetings conducted with the same for SBCC. The cells were found to be taking active initiative to train leadership and officials, and in developing joint efforts with other development actors. Although considerable progress is yet to be made. 

(*Outcomes two and three, namely, improved service delivery and outreach of government programmes, and improved uptake and beneficiary participation towards government sponsored programmes/schemes are beyond the scope of this evaluation.) 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

34 

## **My Village, My Home campaign** 

The Maternal and Child Health Integrated Program (MCHIP) adopted a community-level tool-based programme called My Village, My Home. The objective of this tool is to strengthen service delivery and RI coverage for communities, assess the high drop-out rates and to set up a system of tracking beneficiaries in districts, and to improve the quality of coverage. FLWs use this visual depiction of the immunization status of infants and make use of the community to monitor the immunization coverage of all infants in the locality. The tool is designed in a way that the information on the oldest infant in the community of the respective village is on the bottom row, while that of the younger infants are in the rows upward. The dates of vaccination are written or highlighted in colours in the respective boxes against the names. The advantage of the tool is that it has increased intersectoral collaboration and is a community-friendly tool that can be accessed easily by parents of children, and the ASHA, Anganwadi and ANM workers. 

## 6.2. Effectiveness 

Effectiveness as an evaluation parameter, helps in analysing the extent to which an initiative or intervention has been able to achieve its goals and objectives. It highlights the intervention’s success in terms of producing the desired effects. It helps in understanding, whether the intervention has structural or implementational limitations. In the course of SBCC cell’s evaluation, effectiveness was measured through, their role as institutions for evidence generation, strategic planning, monitoring, training, documentation and analysis, knowledge sharing, cell's contribution in health system strengthening and effectiveness of strategy, material and content developed by the cell. 

We gauged the effectiveness of SBCC with respect to above mentioned categories by interviewing state and district level officials of SBCC cells and corroborated the same through interactions with master trainers, FLW, and community members and observation of training and workshops across the field. 

KEQ 1: To what extent has setting up of SBCC Cells strengthened government systems in bringing about more effective and evidence-based communication initiatives 

## **Category 1** 

The transition from IEC to SBCC although formally occurred between 2014-2015, in practice, the Cells are largely continuing as IEC bureau. Although, an evidence-based communication strategy was designed with support from UNICEF in Assam, there is a lack of evidence generation in terms of regular, updated documentation, particularly in the High Priority aspirational districts, detailed mapping of hard-to-reach areas, Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) at district and block levels, and prioritizing IPC and evidence-building and documentation in tribal belts (hills), Char areas, and tea estates. While the state has worked effectively in evidence based RMNCH+A and RI strategies, there is a potential to further address LODOR areas through innovative communication material and strategies, and through in-depth studies for evidence generation. There was no reported participatory studies or strategy pertaining to M&E plans. The Special Immunization Drive (SID) for immunization coverage of dropout and left-out children was conducted using household display of color-coded stickers (Red, Yellow and Green) based on Immunization status were pasted on houses. The system of stickers ensured easy identification of houses for immunization coverage using community monitoring. Additionally, the HBNC Voucher System worked well and may be considered an evidence-based communication as it helped mothers and FLHWs to be updated on home visits which were documentary evidence. However, there was no mention or observation of the HBNC Voucher system on field. The SBCC Cell has received good support from UNICEF for work on RI, Training and Communication activities. The Har Ghar Dastak campaign has been an effective programme that reached out to LODOR areas through awareness generation, mobilization, and vaccination activities. To a great extent, it can be observed that the SBCC Cell has strengthened the government system specifically in the health-related development, i.e., Immunization and such, but needs to do more to collaborate with government’s efforts on behavioral change at the ground. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

35 

## **Category 2** 

The cells in category two have not been able to focus on evidence generation to develop evidence-based communication initiatives or planning. Though, it is important to note that the national and state programs that the cells contribute to are planned and programmed with an evidence-based focus and are able to identify vulnerable sections of the community which require higher efforts to be equitably integrated in the service delivery process. 

The members of the cells are government health officials who do not have the technical expertise to conduct evidence generation studies and engage in data collection. Further the funding for these cells comes through program budgets which only include enough funds for conducting communications activities such as printing of posters for the said program, and does not take into account other aspects of SBCC such as evidence generation, monitoring, etc. 

## **Category 3** 

The cells participation in primary research is limited. Though within CRU, there was evidence of use of existing secondary data sources (from sources such as NFHS and DHS surveys) for evidence. Further, both cells were able to leverage data collected by WCD’s through its interventions and use it as source of secondary literature. The cells leverage secondary data and evidence generated by line departments such as the WCD who are engaged in implementation activities on field to identify challenges related to demand generation of WASH, child protection and nutrition schemes and services. In this manner, the cells have been able to contribute to developing differentiated communications strategies based on evidence from secondary sources. 

**==> picture [490 x 79] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Research oriented forward looking goals** 

CRU’s (Telangana) main forward-looking goal is to establish itself as a research focused institution. It wishes to focus on primary data collection and drawing in depth behavioral analyses from the data collected. CRU is housed in NIRDPR and is under the leadership of the head of Center of Gender Studies, TISS Hyderabad. Under such a leadership, the cell staff is academically oriented and has a clear understanding of research methodologies. For example, compared to the other state cells, staff of CRU had a distinctly better understanding of the process of evidence generation and the type of resources required to conduct it. 

KEQ 2: To what extent is evidence-based programming effective in identifying and addressing the challenges to demand generation for RI, health, nutrition, and WASH services? 

## **Category 1** 

At this point, the effectiveness of evidence-based programming in RI, health, nutrition, and WASH services cannot be entirely ascertained for the cell as they have not engaged in evidence-generation activities (as was observed on ground during the evaluation). 

## **Category 2** 

The effectiveness of evidence-based programming and identifying and addressing challenges in routine immunization, health, nutrition, and wash practices cannot be ascertained for the cells in this category as they 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

36 

have not been able to engage in evidence generation activities and develop evidence-based programming. (As observed during the evaluation) 

## **Category 3** 

The effectiveness of evidence-based programming and identifying and addressing challenges in routine immunization, health, nutrition, and wash practices cannot be fully ascertained for the cells in this category as they have not been able to engage in primary data collection to generate evidence and develop evidence-based programming. Though through the cells’ success in being able to identify and address the challenges in nutrition and WASH services using secondary sources of evidence suggest a positive effect of evidence-based programming on these processes. 

It is important to note that since the cells in this category are not housed under the health department, they have not contributed to developing evidence-based programming for identifying and addressing the challenges in RI and health services. (As observed during the evaluation) 

KEQ 3: To what extent have the training equipped stakeholders to plan, implement, monitor behavioral change, and demand generation initiatives? 

## **Category 1** 

It was gathered from the evaluation, that there are gaps at two levels in the capacity building of the Cells. One is, that there is a lack of vision to design SBCC interventions in terms of innovative design, programmes, and their implementation, and continued newer ways to contextualize and strategize local health communication interventions. Second, that a shortage of (suitably skilled) staff/Cell officers with competencies on account of a lack of organizational capacity within the Cell has hindered the possibilities of conducting and strengthening the capacity building and improve the quality and effectiveness of SBCC work. Vacant positions at key government positions at the State and district level SBCC cells have only stalled capacity strengthening. The Cell’s existing members are only able to execute the tasks assigned and possible within their capacity and purview of their duties on a regular basis. However, capacity building for SBCC officers and FLHWs to increase their skills and knowledge through periodic trainings is lacking at this point. Even though FLHWs have received training on communication intermittently, there is a gap in scaling up their attitudes, knowledge, awareness, and skills to keep pace with the frequent and evolving health schemes and programmes. As it was found, currently, there are no BRIDGE training and people (including trainers) are no familiar with TARANG. FLHWs are not adequately skilled in conducting mid-media events and IPC, or even interact with communities on deeper and wider social and gender and equity concerns. These would require them to gain skill sets and there are gaps in varying degrees across the FLHWs workforce. As was found on the field, processes of feedback sharing, monitoring, and remedying measures to scale up by the Cells are missing. Therefore, as was observed, the Cells are not yet strengthened in providing refresher’s training. 

**==> picture [483 x 64] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Category 2:** 

The cells have been able to carry out training and capacity building to equip stakeholders to plan and implement behavioural change initiatives. Though the effectiveness of the training differs for each of the cells. For example, in Odisha the training included an interactive workshop to develop the capacity of the frontline health workers in methods of SBCC, whilst the training in UP merely was a presentation of the district’s performance across health indicators and was delivered in a lecture mode. 

Since the cells in this category are housed under the health department, they have easy access to a large cadre of trainees (frontline health workers) who can implement techniques of behaviour change at the field level. Training on monitoring has not happened as the cells in this category have not engaged with monitoring activities. 

## **Category 3:** 

CRU conducts training for district level functionaries such as the IEC consultants within SBM on SBCC and demand generation initiatives. The effectiveness of the training translating into demand generation could not be commented on in absence of supporting research. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

37 

**==> picture [476 x 63] intentionally omitted <==**

In West Bengal the SBCC cell staff have conducted training on SBCC principles such as how an individual can change their behavior while creating domino effects in their immediate environment like family, community, school etc. Similar training has been done for frontline workers like ANM, AWW, ASHA, PRI members, SHG groups and have been equipped to deliver key messages. 

KEQ 4 : How effective is the communication strategy, material and content developed by the SBCC Cells towards demand generation for development schemes and initiatives? 

## **Category 1** 

Most of the health communication strategies and materials for the state are guided/ developed/managed by the IEC/BCC unit related to thematic BCC activities, including a range of communication channels, and designing materials and programmes for vulnerable populations. Key activities carried out by the IEC division are drawn from thematic campaigns of NRHM, including message designing, and material development, and distribution of audio/video and print material/software. In addition to communication, IEC/BCC unit is also responsible for training on BCC/IPC (inter-personal communication). IEC/BCC unit undertakes several activities in the ongoing year as determined in the Annual Action Plans. State Institute of Health and Family Welfare (SIHFW) is entrusted to train in all other subjects/themes except BCC/IPC in the state. The SBCC material must be published in multiple languages considering the cultural and ethnic diversity. Mission Tejaswee on the prevention and control of anemia is an instance of effective communication strategy included awareness generation, advocacy, distribution of IFA tablets to expectant mothers and lactating mothers by ANM. It was found that despite such efforts, there is a lack of funding that is much needed for creating transformative SBCC communication material in minority areas. and LODOR. Conclusively, district level officers and trainers demand communication material for LODOR groups, it was observed that both lack of vision to have wider reach in LODOR combined with insufficient funds has prevented the Cells from disseminating effective communication materials. However, there was no documented evidence demonstrated on lack of fund, during the evaluation. 

**==> picture [468 x 59] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Category 2** 

The cells themselves do not develop a lot of communication material or content. They rely on the material provided by the health department which includes materials such as posters and banners. Sometimes material and content developed by other development agencies such as UNICEF, CHAI and UNDP are also used as part of dissemination of health messaging. For example, the ‘RI wheel’ developed by CHAI is found to be extremely useful by frontline health workers in explaining to new mothers the due date of the next vaccine for their infant. This can be attributed to the lack of funds and dedicated communications cadre including a designer in each cell. The cells’ inability to develop material can be attributed to the lack of funds and dedicated communications cadre including a designer in each cell. The cells in this category are able to contribute to developing a communication strategy at the district level by giving inputs on how dissemination and implementation at the district should be charted out. 

## **Category 3** 

Most of the IEC material has resulted in appropriate and targeted communication at the district level. For instance, the material sent by the national and state governments is made relevant to local contexts by translating the 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

38 

content to local languages and dialects and changing the imagery and social examples to more culturally relevant ones. In the districts, not all SBCC agenda of targeted communication and driving social behaviour change is successful in the district priorities. The evidence-driven communication of SBCC programme often takes a backseat and gradually engagement of issue-based communication design is moving towards an IEC-based approach. Though there is evidence of differentiated district for instance, during COVID, the SBCC cell gave developed a lot of material on how to communicate with communities and designed pamphlets and posters for dissemination. 

## **Saas-Bahu Sammelan** 

The Saas Bahu Sammelan is a state health department's initiative (Madhya Pradesh) rolled out under the directive of the National Rural Health Mission (NRHM). The main objective of this initiative has been to create a sense of bonding and understanding between expectant and new mothers and mothers-in-law to address concerns and misconceptions and myths around child-bearing and postpregnancy phases for new mothers and their child. Through regular meetings, awareness campaigns, and ‘munaadis’, the sammelan is conducted at the village level by Anganwadi workers to encourage pregnant and new mothers and their mothers-in-law to gather and participate in discussions on socially relevant topics, to engage in a healthy discussion in a safe space in address and ask and get clarifications on women’s health, childbirth, personal hygiene, etc., in a communitybased environment. A larger objective of this initiative is to protect the girl child. 

KEQ 5: To what extent have the SBCC Cells improved collaborations, partnerships, and convergence with allied departments at the national and state level/ Developmental agencies/ corporates? 

## **Category 1** 

The state’s SBCC Cells are currently RMNCH+A and RI focused. However, within the SBCC, there is a need for improving coordination between the NHM and DHSFW whereby resources and SBCC activities can be shared and allocated between the health departments where the Cells can have equitable participation in development of the PIPs and pool in resources to IPC than on printing materials in order to make the transition to SBCC more effective, and to strengthen SBCC activities. There is better convergence at the block and district levels which are aligned to WASH and ICDS and engaged in SBCC health communication. Medical college faculties have monitored programmes with the support of UNICEF, although there was no further insight on the same. Although the NGOs work with the support of the NHM in remote and hilly tribal and other socio-economically vulnerable areas, it was found that collaborations and engagements with development agencies and NGOs are limited. As was noted, that boat clinics and community IEC is not yet comprehensively covered. NGOs such as the Voluntary Health Association of India (VHAI) engage in issues such as communicable diseases, Reproductive Maternal Child and Adolescent Health, immunization, food and nutrition, and community participation and ownership through innovative programmes (egs: SALT, KHOJ, Arunoday), the organization does not work very closely with the state Cell. The State cells partner with bilateral and donor agencies such as WHO, UNDP, UNICEF, and JSI. Additionally, the NHM BCC Cell collaborates with a few NGOs and CBOs for communication outreach activities. The DHSFW has had a partnership with the Community Medicine Department of Assam Medical College. 

## **Category 2** 

The cells are housed under the health department which already has a large network of collaborations and partnerships in place which the cells can leverage to their benefit. Though the cells have been able to develop convergence with allied departments and development agencies to carry out implementation of SBCC based programming, there is potential to further strengthen convergence with allied departments which is often restricted by the mutual exclusivity of programs within these departments. The cells also have partnerships with NGOs with whom they collaborate for more effective implementation at the community level as NGOs usually have field level 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

39 

presence. In UP, there was a one-off incidence of a local sugar factory contributing to the health department’s Covid-19 response through its CSR funds. 

## **Category 3** 

The cells in this category are housed under the NIRDPR and WCD. Both departments already have a large network of collaborations and partnerships in place which the cells can leverage to their benefit. Though the cells have been able to develop convergence with allied departments such as WCD for CRU and Department of Panchayats & Rural Development for the cell in West Bengal, and other development agencies to carry out implementation of SBCC based programming, there is potential to further strengthen convergence with other allied departments which is often restricted by the mutual exclusivity of programs within these departments. There is evidence of engagement of the cells with CSOs/NGOs during the pandemic at an implementation level but beyond the pandemic, there has been limited standing partnerships. 

CRU has an added advantage of being housed under a national institute which gives it access to the institutes’ established systems and institutions of service delivery (SIRDs) at the state level too.

## **Conclusion** 

The cells across all three categories were found to be lacking in three out of the four short term outputs mentioned in the theory of change. The cells were not successfully able to establish systems and processes to design, implement and monitor SBCC plans/programs and use evidence for programme planning hence leading to limited evidence, documentation, and completed studies on SBCC. Though it was found that effective dissemination for SBCC program evidence through workshops, media, etc was being taken place. Hence, it can be concluded that within the evaluation criterion of effectiveness, the cells have a major scope of improvement to achieve the first and fourth longer term outcomes in the theory of change namely, institutionalization of SBCC programming through sustainable systemic reforms, and positive policy environment created for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives. 

## 6.3. Efficiency 

Efficiency measures the extent of resource utilization in the optimum, economical and timely manner. It provides justification about resources utilized in the achievement of intervention’s goals. Evaluation of efficiency of an intervention includes critical evaluation of timeliness, economic and operational efficiency. Throughout the SBCC cell evaluation, programme was examined through cell’s functioning within allocated resources and within planned timelines. 

The efficiency of the cell was analysed through insights received by interviewing state, UNICEF consultants (loaned staff) and district level officials of SBCC cells. 

KEQ 1:  Did the SBCC cells efficiently deliver / implement their activities and function within the planned/ allocated monetary resources? 

## **Category 1** 

This has been partially achieved. SBCC activities use funds from the Single Nodal Agency (SNA) and do not have dedicated funds presently, and thus Child Health or Maternal Health are included in their respective sections. As it was found that the SBCC Cell is not duly consulted during the PIP formulation, thus impacting communication activities vis-á-vis the budget . Although budget for FY 2020–2021 increased in Assam, it was mainly allocated for Anemia Free India strategy, a progamme that is conducted through track and act for improved planning and allocations which is at 48 percent. Budgets have increased due better coverage of IFA supplementation and use of ‘Hemoglobin’ meters . It was difficult to ascertain from the evaluation if the budget was utilized anywhere other than RMNCH+A. 

**==> picture [476 x 63] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Category 2** 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

43 

The cells in category two receive their monetary allocation from the respective program for which they have to carry out communications activity; meaning there is no dedicated budget for the cells. Since the state governments are still in the process of transitioning from IEC to SBCC, their understanding of estimated expenditure on communications activities is still guided by an IEC inventory of printing posters and banners with the recent inclusion of activities such as wall painting and mega phoning. 

**==> picture [476 x 63] intentionally omitted <==**

For example, in Odisha even though the budget increased from INR 2 crores in 2011 to INR 200 crores in 2022, the number of programs and initiatives being run by the cell also increased and thus ensured that the deficit was still maintained. Hence, the cells’ efficiency in delivery is limited by inadequate funding which does not account for many aspects of SBCC such as monitoring, research and evidence generation. 

Further, the cells are not allowed to use the funds as they seem fit to carry out SBCC activities rather the department gives out a detailed instruction on which activities to spend how much on. The monitoring of expenditure of this fund is done through checking whether the expenditure target was reached for each item suggested by the department. Hence the cell must work strictly within these guidelines. 

## **Category 3** 

The cell in West Bengal has inadequate financial allocation to efficiently deliver SBCC activities. For example, in the district of Purulia, only 20 schools could be selected for the WASH in school initiative due to limited budget availability. Further, inconsistencies in the budget cycle of SBCC affects the functioning of the cell wherein quarterly releases are delayed by months which affects the planning and programming. Though the SBCC consultant and budget available from UNICEF is a resource which the district cells are able to leverage in the functioning of the cell. 

The cell in Telangana is able to deliver efficiently within the allocated monetary resources as it has a say in the annual budget planning which is done vis-à-vis its annual action plan. Hence, the cell is able to advocate for the budget it would require to efficiently carry out its activities for the year. However, it was mentioned that during Covid-19 response, it has not been able to utilise the available budget and the relevant funds have been returned to UNICEF. 

KEQ 2: Did the SBCC Cells efficiently deliver/implement their activities and function within the planned/allocated human resources? 

## **Category 1** 

This has been partially achieved, as there is shortage of FLWs at the district and block levels, specifically in remote and hard to reach areas, and additionally, the FLWs lack adequate and advance training in building their interpersonal skills. With a larger segment of FLWs in the aging category, and no new recruitment of FLWs currently, there is a wide gap in practicing the skill after training. This also has had an impact on the coverage of immunization which has not reached a hundred percent due to limited outreach and inaccessibility issues. The Cells although short-staffed, have been able to deliver the SBCC activities in a timely manner without delays, particularly prescribed tasks which are delivered by the Cell on a regular and periodic basis, and which are well within the scope of the duties of SBCC officers. 

## **Category 2** 

The cells’ allocation of human resources depends on state to state as the structure of each cell is different and other external factors such as vacancy in positions of health officials in this state also affects this allocation. For example, in Odisha there was only one resource person in the block level cells, whilst in UP there is an issue of vacancy in positions of health officials at district and block level. The capacity to focus on communications activity by these health officials at block, district and state levels is limited as priority is given to the governance and programmatic aspects of their professional role. The need for a dedicated communications cadre at all three levels was strongly felt for better functioning of the cells in respect to human resource utilization. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

44 

## **Category 3** 

The cells can efficiently deliver within the allocated human resources. CRU does not have a mandate to implement the communications strategies it has developed. For the processes of developing strategies and contextualizing IEC and SBCC material, it has adequate human resources and can deliver well within the allocated human resources. For example, having an allocated designer within CRU has proved effective in quickly turning around campaign material at a low cost as compared to hiring designers for every campaign. In West Bengal, effective convergence of block level SBCC staff with frontline workers and Panchayat workers has allowed the cell to deliver efficiently within the allocated human resources. 

## **Empowered frontline health workers for a robust health system** 

**==> picture [74 x 65] intentionally omitted <==**

Eruptions of laughter and loud chatter welcome you into Balrampur Anganwadi centre. Frontline health workers such as anganwadi workers, ASHA workers and ANMs have congregated in the centre for an interaction with the evaluators. The workers are confident, well informed and experienced in bringing about social behavioural change through health messaging and communications. Details of the myriad of health interventions they contribute to are at their fingertips. Their engagement is active, and they voice opinions and suggestions that they have already shared with their superiors. 

There is a lot of focus on empowering frontline health workers in Uttar Pradesh. The frontline health workers are equipped with the latest SBCC material available in the state, such as health educational videos (which are readily accessible in their own personal mobiles), pamphlets, posters and banners on topics such as COVID protocols, hand washing, routine immunization, etc. They also undergo regular training in communications and understanding health indicators in their local context. Most importantly, they are in direct line of communication with their superiors, which includes not just the ANMs but also other health officials such as the chief medical officer and health education officers. This sort of open communication has led to better access to resources for the frontline health workers and has harboured a sense of empowerment in the workers who feel that their voices and opinions. 

KEQ 3: Did the SBCC Cells sufficiently deliver/implement their activities and function within the planned/allocated timelines? 

## **Category 1** 

Even though FLHWs have received training on communication intermittently, there is gap in scaling up their attitudes, knowledge, awareness, and skills to keep pace with the frequent and evolving health schemes and programmes. This also has had an impact on the coverage of immunization which has not reached a hundred percent due to limited outreach and inaccessibility issues. While the Cell works under tight timelines, particularly when there are new SBCC campaigns on RMNCH+A to be delivered or awareness to be shared. During Covid19 and at other times of immunization activities, the Cell has worked beyond its capacity to be able to largely meet the allocated timelines. 

## **Category 2** 

Since the cells are housed under the health department, they are mandated to carry out the communications activities for various health intervention and programs. The cells work on a challenging timeline as they must manage to fulfil their role in long term programs while also organizing various days celebrated by the government to bring about increased awareness regarding health practices and increase of uptake of health services such as Poshan Pakhwada, Swachh Bharat Mission day, international yoga day and world immunization week. This affects the efficiency of the cells’ delivery as it results in reduced time and capacity of the cell to engage in other aspects of SBCC such as evidence generation, monitoring impact and conducting research on behaviour change. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

45 

## **Category 3** 

In West Bengal, the budget cycle of SBCC affects the cell’s efficient delivery within planned timelines. Due to delays in quarterly releases by months, the cell is unable to carry out its activities in a timely manner. These delays affect the planning and programming of the cell and implementation of its activities. For CRU, whilst they are able to more or less deliver efficiently within the plan the timelines, the cell does work on a challenging timeline. The dual responsibility of organizing various days, like, SBM day, celebrated by the government along with regular SBCC activities, act as barriers for functioning within allocated timelines **.** 

## **Conclusion** 

The short term output goals mentioned in the theory change which fall within the ambit of evaluating efficiency are namely availability of a pool of skilled functionaries in effective SBCC programming, monitoring and implementation and increased allocation and utilization of budget/resources for SBCC programming. Across the cells in all three categories, with the exception of the cell in Telangana (CRU), it was found that there is a need for a skilled and dedicated cadre of functionaries with expertise in communications. Further, the same five cells (with CRU as exception) also exhibited a need for increased budget to ensure efficient delivery of SBCC activities. This indicates that the cells have a major scope of improvement to achieve  the first and fourth longer term outcomes in the theory of change, namely, institutionalization of SBCC programming through sustainable systemic reforms, and a positive policy environment created for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives. 

## **Har Ghar Dastak campaign** 

The Har Ghar Dastak Abhiyan launched in 2021 is aimed at vaccinating and covering eligible populations groups (and those in hard-to-reach areas) for the first, second and precaution doses through house-to-house and door-to-door campaigns. The campaign is aimed at old age homes, schools and colleges, including the out-of-school children, and tribal rural communities and workers in brick kilns, etc. The campaign was relaunched as “Har Ghar Dastak campaign 2.0” to accelerate the coverage of COVID19 vaccination across States and districts, widely undertaken by NGOs. It is undertaken by utilizing the local influencers and community leaders to enhance the awareness among communities to take vaccine doses. 

## 6.4. Sustainability 

Evaluation of sustainability of the intervention involves exploring institutional, financial, social, and environmental capacity and capability. It provides crucial insights into the perpetuation or estimated perpetuation of the intervention’s positive impacts in shorter and longer duration. During the evaluation of SBCC cells programme, probability of integration of SBCC programming in state department planning, government’s capacity to carry out SBCC activities independently. Additionally, adequacy of the financial allocations and partnerships to carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells was also evaluated. 

The sustainability of the cell was evaluated through insights received by interviewing state and district level officials of SBCC cells. It was corroborated through interactions with UNICEF SBCC specialists at national and state level. 

KEQ 1. To what extent have the different state departments integrated SBCC programming into its planning and implementation of initiatives? 

## **Category 1** 

Assam was one among the first states to have established an SBCC cell in 2007 brought under the NRHM for converging all health communications at on place. Under the DHSFW, the Cell focuses on communication related 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

46 

to RI to address and eliminate the surrounding misconceptions that have so far constrained the state from attaining full immunization coverage. In this regard, the departments have integrated SBCC programming into its planning and implementation of SBCC initiatives, as it has comprehensively addressed a few critical healthrelated areas within the same programme such as flagship initiatives such as Mission Indradhanush and Mission Tejaswee, RMNCH+A, WASH, and MCH, etc., some key socially relevant issues related to child and adolescent SRH and women’s SRH have not been integrated within the SBCC programmes. However, state departments need further integrate SBCC programming into its planning and implementation more holistically with focus and emphasis on behavior change. 

## **Category 2** 

The transition from IC to SBCC has not fully happened, and has happened to varying degrees, for the cells in category 2. Planning, programming, and implementation of initiatives take into account communications activities only to the extent of IEC. For example, in UP, communications activities are mostly limited to printing of posters and banners. Even in Odisha, which is the best performing sell out of the three in this category, the integration of SBCC programming in planning and implementation of initiatives is restricted by factors such as budget. For example, for the implementation of SBCC activities within a program, a district is only given 2000 rupees to conduct the activities. With the budget being so small, majority of it gets spent on the tried and tested methods of IEC such as printing posters and banners, and almost no budget is left for conduction of SBCC methods such as nukad natak and IPC. 

Though it is important to note that since the cells in category two are housed under the health department in the state government and their work is in alignment with state and national level programs, this allows for a certain level of integration of SBCC programming in the state’s planning and implementation of initiatives, including allocation of funds for SBCC activities from program budget and designating of human resource for communications from within the workforce of the department. 

Amongst the three cells in this category, the integration of SBCC programming in the states planning and implementation of initiatives was lowest in UP. This can be attributed to the lack of an active cell at the state level which would otherwise play the role of advocating for integration of SBCC in state programming. 

## **Category 3** 

States’ shift from IEC to SBCC process has been only partially fulfilled. Cells have not been able to bring the SBCC agenda to the forefront and hence, SBCC programming in state department planning has not been fully integrated. 

In West Bengal, the visibility of the block volunteers at the ground level and regular interactions and updates on SBCC Cell’s activities have widely helped in integration and collaboration, as the cell’s members work with officials from AWW and CDPO related to the WCD, and also with the BMOH and MNB coordinators. The SBCC cell’s engagement with District Social Welfare officers rather than with Zilla Parishad has impacted integration to a certain extent. 

CRU in Telangana has been able to establish itself as a one stop shop for support and technical guidance provision on communications activities and through its work, has been able to help departments understand the importance of SBCC. Hence, various state departments reach out to the cell for support to develop communications strategy for programs such as SBM and JJM out of their own understanding of the significance of having a communications strategy for their respective programs. Hence, it can be said that CRU has been able to contribute to the integration of SBCC programming in the state department planning. 

KEQ 2. Does the government (at the state and district-level) have the capacity to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently? 

## **Category 1** 

The findings indicate that there is potential for the government to be able to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC cells independently. The cells housed under the health department, are working on attaining comprehensive coverage of RI across rural and urban areas. However, the state’s capacity building will include increasing the workforce strength of FLWs at the district, and down to the block level. The SBCC cells need a more socially aware and SBCC-oriented (not only IEC-oriented) personnel, inclusion in the programme budget for mainstreaming SBCC and gender equity, and leadership from senior government officials and other government resources. Only if the cells can actively endeavor to build capacity of the SBCC for officials within the departments, it can accelerate the integration of SBCC into its programming and carry out the activities. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

47 

However, greater accountability, coordination and convergence with SBCC-orientation and streamlining budget allocations can enable the government to take the SBCC activities forward. At this point, it was observed that the government both at the state and district level can independently carry out the commonplace activities in so far as the health and communication of SBCC mandates are concerned. 

## **Category 2** 

For cells in category two, the findings indicate that there is potential for the government to be able to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC cells independently. These cells are housed under the health department, which gives them a few advantages such as access to a large cadre of health workers positioned down to the block level, inclusion in the program budget, governance and leadership from senior government officials, and other government resources. With the cells actively advocating for and building capacity in SBCC for officials within the health department, the department has been able to develop its capacity to integrate SBCC into its programming and be able to successfully implement it at the field level. 

Though in UP, there has been a lack of ownership and accountability from the government’s side, especially at the state level, due to which the potential for the government to be able to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC cells independently is not fulfilled and has remained limited. 

**==> picture [476 x 63] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Category 3** 

In both states, the government (at the state and district-level) have been reported to have limited capacity to adequately carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently. They have limited technical knowledge / expertise. 

Though in West Bengal, the cells work very closely with district administration and are flexible to adopt to changes. The block volunteers and district coordinator are selected through district recruitment processes.  And any requests from district administration for IEC support is well received by the department. 

In CRU’s case, its financial allocation comes solely from UNICEF. Hence, the government presently has limited opportunity to support adequate financial allocations and partnerships to successfully carry out activities independently. Though each department and programme have a budget for IEC/SBCC activities, this only covers the cost of development of material. 

KEQ 3. Do the Cells and their implementation structures (at the state and district-level) have adequate financial allocations and partnerships to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently? 

## **Category 1** 

The existing partnerships as was observed, are not yet strengthened for better communication strategies.; Additionally, a lack of convergence with other relevant stakeholders for collaboration, implementation, and sustainability of SBCC strategies is currently not robust in the state. The SBCC cell under NHM receive financial support through the State’s PIP, as the NHM PIP is the main source of funding. IEC-related planning has been the aggregate of the budget of IEC under the umbrella of the NHM at the state level. Moreover, the funding for these cells comes through program budgets which are largely channelized for RI communications activities.  The budget is managed through collaborative efforts of DMO, DCM, DEMO, etc. all work together on PIPs. Cell and system strengthening can be achieved through sufficient budgeting and availability of independent financial resources for the cell. There is thinning of budgets at the time of national review of the state PIPs, which leads to communication activities being placed under innovative schemes within their respective budget heads. There is a single-nodal agency where funds come in and which are then distributed from the source to other departments. streamlining budget allocations can enable the government to take the SBCC activities forward. Community engagement although supported strongly by community influencers, it is largely lacking due to shortage of human resources, coordinated and convergent efforts between departments, lack of intersectoral convergence, and community engagement are factors that can scale up and sustain through a more empowered community-based interventions and community-led initiatives with support from the state and district cells. There was a consensus among district officials that the Cell has not disseminated adequate funds to carry forward SBCC activities in 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

48 

areas and among communities where urgently needed. However, whether it is a paucity of funds or a lack of optimal usage of funds at appropriate contexts, was difficult to ascertain from the evaluation. Partnerships too are limited and most definitely have not been utilized to the fullest. 

## **Category 2** 

The funds for the cells in category two are allocated from within the budget of the health department. In most cases, the budget for SBCC/IECC activities is accounted for from the program budget itself. All three cells within this category spoke about experiencing limitations in working within the allocated funds. The cells also faced an issue in the way the fund expenditure is being monitored, which is through checking whether the cells have reached their spending target as allocated by the health department at the state level. This method was pointed out to be rigid and resulting in a top-down approach which did not consider the difference in the local contextual reality across the state. 

The cells have adequate partnerships with other development agencies and other departments through the collaborations of the health department. Development agencies such as CHAI, JHPIEGO and UNDP also develop communications material for various aspects of health programming. Such material contributes to the larger objective to reach out to as many members of society especially those from vulnerable populations. 

## **Category 3** 

The cell in West Bengal has inadequate financial allocation to effectively carry out SBCC activities. Delays in the budget cycle of SBCC affects the functioning of the cell wherein quarterly releases are delayed by months which affects the planning and programming. There is an instance where due to limited budget availability, the cell in Purulia had to choose only 20 schools for WASH in School initiative in an entire district. Though the SBCC consultant and budget available from UNICEF is a resource which the district cells are able to leverage in the functioning of the cell. 

The cell in Telangana has adequate financial allocations as it has a say in the annual budget planning which is funded wholly by UNICEF. However, it was mentioned that during Covid-19 response, it has not been able to utilize the available budget and the relevant funds have been returned to UNICEF. Though, it is important to note that since the budget is funded completely by UNICEF, the sustainability of this source it's limited till the time UNICEF continues to extend this support. The department under which the cell is housed (NIRD PR), does not allocate any part of the funding for the cell which has been noted to be an important indicator of sustainability. In view of the lack of funding from the state department under which the cell is housed, increases the dependency on UNICEF supported resources and budget. 

SBCC cells need to develop and/or strengthen partnerships with the department of health (in both states), WCD (in Telangana) and Panchayati Raj (in West Bengal) department for implementation of SBCC activities. It would allow increased leveraging of available IEC/ SBCC budgets with allied departments. Additionally, NGOs should be involved in implementation of SBCC activities **.** 

## **Conclusion** 

Against sustainability as an evaluation criterion, there are initiatives being taken for achievement of all three outputs mentioned in the theory of change, namely, training of leadership and officials on SBCC programming reflecting the shift from IEC to SBCC, availability of structured plan for interactions with community members,and field functionaries, and availability of relevant job aids, tools, AV materials, prototypes, etc for SBCC. While progress is being made in each of these, they are yet to be fully achieved. In the case of Odisha as an example, government ownership is high and hence the cells are able to actively advocate for and build capacity in SBCC for officials within the health department, integrate SBCC into its programming, and be able to successfully implement it at the field level. Hence, with the achievement of these outputs, the cells will be able to contribute to the moving towards the longer term outcomes namely, institutionalization of SBCC programming through sustainable systemic reforms, and positive policy environment created for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives. 

## 6.5. Coherence 

Evaluation of Coherence of the intervention helps in gauging the degree of support, coordination or undermining, hinderance witnessed with other interventions and policies of the stakeholders. In current interdependent and interrelated world, it becomes essential to explore the impact of internal and external stakeholders, their interventions and policies, to ensure effective and efficient results. Here, evaluation of SBCC cells programme involved examining coherence based on partnerships and integration of SBCC in state programming. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

49 

We evaluated interlinkage of SBCC cells with other development partners and its alinement with interventions being carried out by state for improving health systems by interviewing state, district level officials of SBCC cells and consultants of various development partners and NGOs. 

KEQ 1. How do the activities/interventions of the SBCC Cells interlink and synergize with the existing/other programme/initiatives by other relevant actors (NGOs and development partners and other sector players)? 

## **Category 1** 

Partnerships with NGOs as was found have proved effective during Covid-19. However, the NGOs on a regular basis are not actively involved, nor exchange any knowledge or work on SBCC activities. This is despite the fact that NGOs are also working closely with communities on RI programmes, as do the SBCC Cells. However, at this point, there is no concrete linkage of NGOs with the SBCC cells especially related to more focus -driven and pointed work on women’s health, gender and equity. NGOs such as the Voluntary Health Association of India (VHAI) engage in issues such as communicable diseases, Reproductive Maternal Child and Adolescent Health, immunization, food and nutrition, and community participation and ownership through innovative programmes (egs: SALT, KHOJ, Arunoday), the organization does not work very closely with the state Cell. However, they too do not specifically work on gender and equity as a distinct or special attention aspect of their work. It was noted that NGOs are open to collaborating with the state cells to work on the ground level with communities (and FLWs) for wider outreach of SBCC and look forward to convergence. At the state level, there is no mapping of NGO partners is not yet carried out at the local levels, even as it has a limited number of partners and unskilled resources. Most importantly, the state faces challenges in reaching or directly working with communities in sensitive areas or hard-to-reach areas. In case of RI activities, greater institutional support will be further needed. Although the NGOs work with the support of the NHM in remote and hilly tribal and other socio-economically vulnerable areas, it was found that collaborations and engagements with development agencies and NGOs are limited. As was noted, that boat clinics and community IEC is not yet comprehensively covered and is only in its nascent stages (boat clinic concept is one of the good practices from Bangladesh which Assam intends to replicate in the state’s char areas and LODOR). The State cells partner with bilateral and donor agencies such as WHO, UNDP, UNICEF, and JSI. Additionally, the NHM BCC Cell collaborates with a few NGOs and CBOs for communication outreach activities. The DHSFW has had a partnership with the Community Medicine Department of Assam Medical College. 

**==> picture [476 x 50] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Category 2** 

The cells engage with various development actors through the collaborations that the health departments have such as with NGOs, International Development Agencies like UNDP, CHAI, BMGF, etc. to ensure that they delve into specific areas for e.g., youth mobilization, immunization, etc. comprehensively. There are collaborative efforts between the cells and these development agencies to develop new material and seek technical expertise. The implementation aspect of the programs is carried out through government mechanisms already in place. The development agencies leverage these resources and mechanisms to aid and enhance the government work. 

## **Category 3** 

Though CRU mostly works in the area of WASH, and hence has convergence with Swachh Bharat Mission and Jal Jeevan Mission. It also has interlinkage with other departments such as WCD and has contributed to programs such as Poshan Abhiyan. CRU does not have any ongoing interlinkage with other development actors and NGOs. Though during Covid-19 pandemic, they did leverage collaborations with NGOs for on file dissemination of Covid protocols. 

In West Bengal, regarding interdepartmental convergence, the cell collaborates with the health and sanitation departments, and with district Information and Culture officer. The cell does not have any ongoing interlinkage with other development actors and NGOs in the state, despite the presence of multiple such development actors working in the field of health and other related fields such as nutrition. Since there is no convergence or collaboration amongst these actors including with the cell, there is duplication of effort on multiple fronts as each actor is working in silos **.** 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

50 

KEQ 2. How do the SBCC Cells interventions align/synergize/fit with other interventions being carried out by the state for improving health systems? 

## **Category 1** 

Overall, the SBCC Cell is striving to align to its interventions in health objectives. Programmes such as Mission Indradhanush and Mission Tejaswee, and Anemia Free India have been aligned to the objectives of immunization coverage strengthening in priority districts. This has also been largely possible due to the structure of the cell being housed in the NHM as well as the DHSFW. The Health Communication in Assam is implemented by the District Extension Media Officer (DEMO), (now the state Cold Chain officer under NHM. The other members include the DHSFW at the district level, Block Community Mobilizer (BCM), NHM and Health Educator (HE)/Block Extension Educator, DHSFW at the blocks. ASHA and ASHA Supervisors are the main frontline cadre involved in informing communities on available health services, disseminating health messages and assisting in utilization of health services.
