Service delivery at the state level have comprised various innovations towards reduction of mortality, such as through the Rural Health Practitioners (RHP), Boat Ambulance and Clinic (still in the discussion phases and was not heard of from participants in the study, except for one SBCC officer and participant), Mobile Medical Unit (MMU), ASHA Radio Program, Sarathi-104 (Health Information Helpline & Complaint Redressal system), etc. However, on ground it was found that accessibility of these in remote and hard-to-reach areas such as Chars, were difficult, and still a major concern. The gaps have been addressed and reduced in Reproductive Maternal Newborn Child and Adolescent Heath (RMNCH+A), although services and knowledge and awareness by the health facilities and their utilization need a further scale up. However, health systems need to assess and address the existing communication gaps in priority districts and aspirational districts, in LODOR areas, tea gardens, Char areas, and minority settlements which have precarious, cross-cutting health and social behavior change issues such as early marriage, teenage pregnancy, and school dropout holistically and meticulously. These are factors which are also entrenched in mindsets, ignorance, and poverty of the communities. 

## **Category 2** 

The cells in category two are highly aligned to the interventions being carried out by the state for improving health systems. Since the cells are housed under the health department, they are essential parts of the programming and implementation plan of various interventions brought about at state and national level. For example, the cells have contributed to spreading inclusive messaging on various state and national level health priorities such as Mission Indra Dhanush, Dastak Sanchari, Measles and rubella awareness, RI, postnatal care, home based care for young and new-born children and institutional delivery. 

Since the cells’ functioning occurs within the workings of the health department, the cells can leverage the department’s key resources such as frontline health workers who act as the last mile connection with vulnerable populations on field. Further, the cells can enhance the functioning of the health department through introduction and advocacy for novel and inclusive methods of communication which lead to social behavior change and contribute to a significant development from the earlier practice of mere distribution of IEC material. 

## **Category 3** 

Since the cell are not housed within the health department, they have limited alignment to core health programs. Though, they contribute to health systems strengthening by developing communications strategy for programs supporting health initiatives such as nutrition and sanitation programs. For example, CRU in Telangana has contributed to flagship programs such as SBM, JJM and Poshan Abhiyan. For the cell in West Bengal, the expected area of focus is ICDS for Nutrition and the four largest schemes viz., Kanyasree, Rupasree, Lakshmir Bhandar and Social Welfare schemes such as old age and widow pension. 

## **Conclusion** 

For the program to have high coherence to the allied policy space and stakeholders, the theory of change suggests achievement of two outputs, namely, joint programmes planned and organised with allied sectors and meetings conducted with the same for SBCC, and availability of a network of CSOs/CBOs engaged in demand generation and behaviour change activities. It was found that the cells in Telangana, Assam and West Bengal have not been able to develop a steady network of CSOs/CBOs/NGOs due to which they have not been successful in initiating many joint ventures with allied sector actors. Whilst, in Odisha, U.P. and M.P., it was found that cells are able to leverage the network of other development actors and engage in joint planned programmes. Hence, it can be said for Odisha, U.P. and M.P. that they are closer to achieving the long term outcome goals 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

51 

mentioned in the theory of change namely, institutionalization of SBCC programming through sustainable systemic reforms, and positive policy environment created for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives. 

## **SBCC programming with effective leadership** 

The ownership and leadership of SBCC activities at district level from the government’s side in West Bengal has kept the SBCC cell relevant and active. The SBCC cell workers both at the district and block levels are well recognized by the relevant leadership who ensure that all stakeholders are working within a collaborative network. Further, the cell is housed within the Zilla Parishad under the leadership of the ADM and ICDS project officer. Receiving guidance and leadership from senior officials of two departments results in effective convergence at the district level. Convergence also exists between block level SBCC staff and frontline workers. The block volunteers of SBCC cell work very closely with Panchayat workers, ANM, ASHA and AWW at the village/GP level. 

Further, availability of a senior SBC specialist from UNICEF (loaned staff)- The presence of a specialized staff is found to give technical guidance in leading conversations with district administration, forging partnerships (with NSS, with private partners during COVID to leverage masks, sanitizers etc.) and leveraging available IEC budgets with allied departments (like funds for COVID IEC available with BMOH 

## 6.6. Gender and Equity 

From the six evaluated states, it was found that although gender and equity are cross-cutting and a critical component of the SBCC, in practice, the Cells in the states have not yet mainstreamed gender equity. Neither are the Cells gender-cognizant while designing SBCC activities, as was noted under Relevance and Effectiveness across all the categories. Although, the SBCC progammes have principles of gender and equity in theory, at present, the gender and equity as a dimension and intersectional pillar of the SBCC is skewed across the states both in thought and practice. For instance, the evaluation in Telangana finds that gender and equity is institutionalized within the ambit of academics; whereas in other states, gender and equity are far from academic institutionalization; in fact, they are not even well-embedded within the SBCC practice. Most of the state cells have overlooked the gender and equity dimension, whereas, in some state level (and even district level cells), officials who were cognizant of the need for deeper enquiries and assessment of gender issues, simply do not have the necessary human resources (neither in workforce strength), nor mindset or evolving attitudes), and neither a dedicated (gender-responsive) budget. We gather that this gap has created a larger caveat at the district and block level where FLWs interactions with communities is narrow, and restricted to the prescribed SBCC activities.  The SBCC Cells have given attention to WASH services and RMNCH+A , and hygiene and sanitation, nutrition, and women’s health, but have not attended to more fundamental issues of child protection, safeguarding of the rights of adolescent girls, and women. . In this context, the Rashtriya Kishor Swastha Abhijan and ARSH (national programmes for adolescent SRH and holistic development) are missing in any of the states during the consultations). 

## **Relevance** 

Under relevance, it was found that across the 3 categories covering six states, Gender and equity are largely peripheral to most of the SBCC interventions. Nutrition and child survival with the support of community mobilisation have been focus areas, but not as such gender-centric or gender-aware. There is no advocacy at the state and district level on issues of child marriage and adolescent SRH, although the health department is overwhelmed at the number of cases of teen pregnancy and early (child) marriage being reported. Parallelly, in some states, male child preference continues to be prevalent among communities in categories 1 and 2. In the states in category 3, as the cells are housed in departments which are other than the health department, the 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

52 

programmes do not entirely include or focus on gender and equity in its programmatic planning. Whereas, in West Bengal, the state cell housed under WCD, have gender-focused interventions but do not have any specific programmes on equity for vulnerable groups such as tribal populations, people with disabilities, and other poor and marginal communities. 

## **Effectiveness** 

Under effectiveness in category 1, programmes like Mission Tejaswee are anemia-related for women, girls and new mothers, these are only part of women’s health which do not include a gender-inclusive dialogue on the household dynamics of food and nutrition, or women’s and girls’ need for timely eating or eating ‘well’. In category 2, it was found that programmes such as the Adolescent Empowerment Programme (AEP) was implemented across 16 districts under the leadership of District Collectors and the Department of Women and Child Development (DWCD). The states do not yet have adequate and (gender-cognizant) field functionaries to focus on adolescent empowerment, particularly as there is a lack of gender-targeted interventions coupled with lack of literacy in parts of the regions. In Category 3, although there is a lack of stakeholder interventions, and the demand and need of the states is based on contextual requirements of diverse vulnerable populations. Although, West Bengal has ICDS for Nutrition and Social Welfare schemes such as old age and widow pension, there is no specific programmes that are centered on gender and equity. In Mayurbhanj district in Odisha, the Cell has an equity-focused approach wherein they focus on IPC to develop trust building relationships with community members. There is a lack of stakeholder interventions, and the demand and need of the states is based on contextual requirements of diverse vulnerable populations. 

## **Efficiency** 

The overall shortage of resources in Category 1as we gather, has not affected the efficiency of the SBCC objectives, but has largely hindered a gender-transformative approach to address and reduce gender gaps, such as social practices of early marriage or school dropouts. In category 2, there is no specific budget allocation for gender and equity, as the Cell is only part of programmatic planning. Hence, despite a programmatic focus on vulnerable including women and children, there are no specific interventions for the same. We gather that prevalent gender disparities will continue to perpetuate without timely SBCC intervention. In categ ory 3, gender and equity in Telangana is constrained due to lack of separate monetary allocation which also impedes the staff from envisioning gender and equity-focused programmes. In West Bengal, although gender is identified as a key dimension of the Cell’s activities, equity-based planning is missing, and resources are used only partially mainly on other SBCC activities. 

## **Sustainability and Coherence** 

We gather from the study that Sustainability and Coherence are far from being attainable at this point, as we have in the aforementioned sections, concluded that gender and equity are the weak and missing links as was found under relevance and effectiveness. In this direction, largely the advantage of the states in Category 3 is that the programmatic focus is on women and children, which can serve as a good motivation for the state cell to have inclusion in its SBCC agenda – tribals, urban poor, and LODOR in its future activities. Reaching vulnerable groups through house-to-house surveys, a mix of communication approaches, including base line studies which are critical for equity, gender and social inclusion. With no gender-responsive budget but for states like West Bengal, advocacy and establishment of the state adolescent cell is of no added value if there is SBCC activity on equity and inclusion to assess the outcome of an adolescent cell on girls and women in tribal and economically vulnerable communities. 

Conclusively, gender equity must be holistically as well as granularly integrated into the SBCC strategy to make health-related communication more effective, and to trigger a dialogue around equity and gender relations and roles. From budgeting to mainstreaming, gender and equity needs to be entrenched within the SBCC objectives more firmly, to increase the accessibility of SBCC interventions to large numbers of under-served and marginalized populations of women, girls and even men and adolescent youth. 

## **Conclusion** 

Gender and inclusion focused integrated SBCC plans/programmes as an output has not been fully achieved by any of the cells across all three categories. Since, public health is the larger ambit within which the SBCC program functions, there is a programmatic focus on aspects of gender inclusion and equity such as maternal health and increasing health information access for LODOR communities. But, no specific focus was found on gender and equity while planning communications activities relating to SBCC programming. Hence, there is limited success of integrating a gender and equity approach in achievement of the longer term outputs namely, institutionalization 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

53 

of SBCC programming through sustainable systemic reforms, and positive policy environment created for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives. 

**Equity focused SBCC programming** 

In Odisha SBCC programming is being done in a manner which focuses on equity and local contexts. For example, a large percentage of the population in Odisha are tribals. Hence SBCC programming is done in such a manner that it considers various socioeconomic realities of such vulnerable communities such as religious beliefs, social mores, difference in language and dialect and imagery that they associate with. This results in having to develop SBCC material which is slightly different in each district. The SBCC cells are responsible for this process of making the material relevant in the local context. For example, Mayurbhanj has a tribal population of more than 50% of its total population. The frontline health workers in this district have been given extensive training on how to reach out to hard to reach and vulnerable communities. Jaya Sahu, an ASHA worker, tells us how one community in the area assigned to her had no uptake of immunization services when she started out 10 years ago. This community had various misbeliefs regarding the effects of immunization and hence were weary towards it. With the introduction of SBCC methods, Jaya Understood the importance of strengthening interpersonal communication with the members of this community and to have an empathetic ear when they shared their fears regarding vaccination. She was able to build relationships of trust with these members and has managed to get 100% immunization within the said community. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

54 

## Conclusion 7. 

The SBCC cells program was initiated to help state government departments to transition from IEC to SBCC. Though this transition is not yet complete for any of the six states evaluated in this study, the inception of the cells has got the ball rolling in the right direction. The state and district level officials are now familiar with the term ‘SBCC’ and are aware of its basic conceptual definition and how it differs from IEC. They are also aware of the novel methods being used in SBCC such as miking, interpersonal communication, leveraging key influencers, etc. The SBCC program has contributed to health systems strengthening in these six states by bringing about a change in the way health, ICDS and PRI officials approach and engage with community members. 

It can be said that the program is operating in alignment with all four desired outcomes mentioned in the theory of change . Evidence suggests that the cells are contributing and moving towards outcomes one and four which are creation of sustainable government systems for SBCC programming and creation of positive policy environment for SBCC programming across sectoral development initiatives, respectively by achieving, to varying degree, some of the shorter term output goals such as having leadership and officials trained on SBCC methods, and establishing systems and processes to design and implement SBCC programs. The scope of this evaluation does not include assessing outcome two and three which are namely improved demand and outreach of government programs and improved uptake and community participation towards government sponsored programs/schemes. 

It was found that the model of cell in Telangana (CRU) under category 3 was functioning in a manner most efficient to integrate SBCC programming in state planning and implementation of initiatives. As opposed to all the other cells, CRU has managed to establish itself as one stop shop for all communications activities in the state which allows the direction of pursuit for CRU to be such that the departments approach the cell for support out of their own understanding of the significance of having a communications strategy for their respective programs. In this way, CRU has been able to market itself as a center of technical expertise in communications which supports various departments in developing communications strategies. Further, it was found that CRU’s mandate to only operate at the state level acts as an advantage as its resources can be focused on strategizing, whilst implementation remains within the ambit of the state departments. This also ensures that the cell is able to function effectively in the role of providing technical expertise on communication within a smaller pool of human resources and budget allocation. It is important to note that though CRU’s model works best, it is still heavily dependent on UNICEF for its budget allocation. For the sustainability of the cell, CRU should advocate with NIRDPR for fund allocation from within the department’s budget. 

The SBCC cells’ functioning was found to have high **relevance** and alignment with the national and state priorities by contributing to building the communications strategy for flagship programs of departments under which they are housed. The cells have been seen to be flexible and adaptable to evolving needs in the community such as the incidence of Covid-19 pandemic. Further, to varying degrees, the cells have been able to leverage existing systems and institutions of service delivery such as conduction of on field implementation through the cadres of frontline health workers; and have been able to have the presence and guidance of senior government officials in creating the **leadership and governance** structure of the cell.  But, due to various limitations such as inadequate **finance** , challenging timelines, a **workforce** which does not specialize in communications or behavior change and lack of inter-departmental convergence, the **effectiveness** and **efficiency** of the **service delivery** by the cell has been negatively affected. The cells have been only partially successful in fulfilling their role as institutions of evidence generation, monitoring and evaluation, documentation and analysis. Although the cells were established to bring about a change in the way communications is planned, programmed and implemented, a majority of them ended up playing the role of a supportive unit to the state’s IEC activities. The aspects that the cells are working to successfully deliver in are training and capacity building, strategic planning, knowledge sharing and increasing awareness of community members by disseminating health **information** and positive social practices. 

Most of the cells have been able to foster an environment of convergence and collaboration with relevant departments and with other development actors such as IDAs, NGOs and CSOs. Though it is important to note that the need for even higher convergence (especially intersectoral convergence such as a collaboration between health and education department) was noted from the findings across all six states. Through these collaborations, the cells are able to have a higher contribution to integrating SBCC into state programming. Hence, it can be concluded that the cells have **coherence** with institutions and interventions of health and related fields, but not intersectoral. 

The cells have a considerable scope of scaling up and being fully integrated in the public health system of the country. However, to ensure the program’s **sustainability** , government ownership and accountability of the cells 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

55 

is of utmost importance. The first step in this process would be for the government to have a complete shift from IEC to SBCC in its attitude and approach. Following which, the government must conduct regular capacity development workshops and training in communications and behavior change methods for its officials who also play the role of communications officers. This would improve the quality of SBCC material being developed, lead to better planning and programming resulting in more technically sound communications strategies. 

It was found that although **gender and equity** are cross-cutting and a critical component of the SBCC, in practice, the Cells in the states have not yet mainstreamed gender and equity, and neither are there any gender focused programme beyond a basic programmatic focus on gender within health programs. For example, focus on gender in programs such as Poshan Abhiyan and RMNCHA+. A gender-transformative approach is thus a critical requirement to address social norms, practices in diverse social and economic contexts by the state and district cells. Though, through the programmatic focus on gender and equity within health programs, vulnerable populations such as young others from lower socio-economic strata and those in LODOR communities are included in the state programming. 

In conclusion, it can be said that the SBCC cells program **kickstarted the transition of communications activities from IEC to SBCC** . The program's contribution has been significant and in many of the states been the first SBCC intervention initiated. Though the progress has been slow over the past programmatic cycle, it is important to understand that to bring about a change not just in methods but in the approach, understanding and attitude towards communications is a mammoth task. The very principles of SBCC can be applied here to see the slow progress not as a sign of inefficiency and ineffectiveness of the program, but rather as a **work in progress status for the cells** . The cells require guidance and support for at least one more programmatic cycle whilst they figure out their transition strategy to move from being UNICEF supported to completely government owned. **We strongly suggest UNICEF to continue its investment** and support in the program during this period. 

Following are a few recommendations to increase the relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, sustainability, and coherence of the SBCC cells and to streamline the goals, efforts and resources being invested in the program. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

56 

## 8. Policy implications and recommendations 

The objective of this evaluation exercise was to understand the SBCC Cells’ performance towards maximizing utilization of allocated resources and augmenting the results of the health systems strengthening initiatives and other cross sectoral themes of social and health determinants at the state and district level. A qualitative research design was adopted for the same. Findings from this study have provided insights into the status of the existing SBCC cells and identified certain areas for improvement in its functioning. 

The suggestive recommendations for strengthening the functioning of SBCC cells are **based on the findings from study, field observations, consultations with stakeholders.** 

Confidence in the generalizability of the findings and recommendations is enhanced by the comprehensive strategy and design used for the **selection of the states and arena of stakeholders interviewed as a part of this evaluation** . 

## **Recommendation 1:** 

|**Strengthen existing state level cells**|Responsible Organization: Department under<br>which cell is housed in states|
|---|---|
|Priority: High|SBC SS indicator: Leadership and Governance|
|Time frame: 6 – 12 months|Recommendation for category: I, II and III|



## **Rationale:** 

As noted under relevance evaluation criteria (Relevance KEQs), among all the evaluated states, presence of an active state SBCC cell ensured high relevance of SBCC activities to flagship programs, and priorities of the national and state governments. It also enabled collaboration among departments, development partners and NGOs for various SBCC activities. However, the state governments have not taken full ownership of the cells. In states like UP and West Bengal, the state level cells are defunct and SBCC activities were being conducted mostly at the district level.  Hence, it is recommended that government should strengthen the state level SBCC cells for better implementation of SBCC activities in the state and advocacy for adoption of SBCC approach in state government programming. The state SBCC cell would act as a one stop solution for all the SBCC activities in the state and converge the impact of district level cells. 

## **Action points for State:** 

- ➢ Government should strengthen the state level SBCC cells by setting a clear mandate for its roles and responsibilities vis-à-vis the structure of the state department under which the cell is housed. 

- ➢ The state cell should enable communication capacity building for health workforce and provide technical assistance to department it is housed under and other allied departments. 

- ➢ The state cell must assign, but not be limited to, a nodal officer (preferably a BCC expert), a graphic designer, a M&E expert, a communication/ language expert for contextualization of SBCC material to local languages and cultural preference and imagery. 

- ➢ The state cell should coordinate with the district level officials and FLWs and conduct SBCC activities effectively and efficiently at district and block levels. 

## **Recommendation 2:** 

|**Prioritize key areas for provision of technical**<br>**expertise**|Responsible Organization: UNICEF|
|---|---|
|Priority: High|SBC SS indicator: Workforce and service<br>delivery|
|Time frame: 6 – 12 months|Recommendation for category: I, II and III|



UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

57 

## **Rationale:** 

The evaluation found that district level engagement by UNICEF in form of district level loaned consultant was limited to contribution towards capacity building and SBCC repository management. Their role at the district level can be taken over by a health / PRI official, already placed at the district. However, states are yet to make the transition from IEC to SBCC. In view of this, UNICEF should continue its assistance to the states, but with focused engagement. To ensure focused and effective delivery of SBCC, we strongly recommend that UNICEF should concentrate efforts and resources at the state level only through the continued placement of an UNICEF consultant. As evident in states such as Odisha, Telangana and Madhya Pradesh, the presence of a state level consultant who is able to advocate and provide technical expertise at the state level. In the case of Odisha and Madhya Pradesh, a cascade effect of the contribution of the state consultant resulting in increased effectiveness of service delivery was observed even at the district level. 

## **Action points for UNICEF:** 

- ➢ Technical expertise – Support from UNICEF can be instrumental to reduce the gap that currently exists within SBCC programming, particularly related to gender disparities, prejudices, and ill-practices of early marriage, empowerment of girls and safeguarding sexual and reproductive health. UNICEF should continue to extend its support in SBCC activities across the cell through provision of technical expertise. To do so, UNCEF must continue to place a consultant in the state level cells in all six states whose main responsibility would be to set and implement the transition plan for at least one more programmatic cycle of five years. The consultant must have domain expertise in social behavior change and communication. 

- ➢ During the next programmatic cycle, whilst the cells have the guidance of the UNICEF loaned SBCC expert, the focus of the cell should be to develop a clear transition strategy. This strategy should be developed to cover, but not be limited to, aspects of financial resource management human resource management, methods of evidence generation, monitoring and evaluation framework, community engagement and inclusion of gender and equity lens in SBCC programming (expanded in Recommendation 3). This would enable a smooth transition from SBCC activities being UNICEF supported to becoming fully owned by the state government. 

At the end of the next programmatic cycle of five years, a similar evaluation should be conducted to assess the progress of the transition of the state and decide future course of action including the decision to continue stationing of the UNICEF consultant at the state level cell. 

## **Recommendation 3:** 

|**Develop SOPs for SBCC cells at state and**<br>**district level**|Responsible Organization: UNICEF|
|---|---|
|Priority: Medium|SBC SS indicator: Service Delivery|
|Time frame: 12 - 18 months|Recommendation for category: I, II and III|



## **Rationale:** 

Since the state departments under which the cell is housed have not fully transitioned from IEC to SBCC, they require technical support to effectively deliver SBCC activities (Sustainability KEQ 2). As suggested earlier, an UNICEF SBCC consultant who is a technical expert in SBCC programming should continue to provide support for another programmatic cycle of five years. In addition to the consultant, whose engagement is for a fixed period of time, the development of principle guiding documents in the form of clearly laid out SOPs would ensure a continued support and source of knowledge of methods of SBCC for the said departments. Hence, UNICEF is recommended to develop SOPs on various aspects of the functioning of cell to effectively deliver SBCC activities. 

## **Action point for UNICEF:** 

- ➢ Development of SOPs - UNICEF should develop SOPs for the SBCC cells at state and district level which will work as principle guiding documents for state department communications officers to effectively carry out SBCC activities after the withdrawal of UNICEF support post the next programmatic cycle. The SOPs should cover, but not be limited to, the following topics- 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

58 

**1.** Financial resource management 

**2.** Human resource management 

**3.** Methods of evidence generation 

**4.** Monitoring and evaluation framework 

**5.** Community engagement 

**6.** Inclusion of gender and equity lens in SBCC programming 

## **Recommendation 4:** 

|**Adopt a systematic approach to human**<br>**resource management**|Responsible Organization: Department under<br>which cell is housed (primary role) and UNICEF<br>(secondary role)|
|---|---|
|Priority: High|SBC SS indicator: Workforce|
|Time frame: 12-18 months|Recommendation for category: I, II and III|



## **Rationale:** 

- It was highlighted that there was shortfall in availability of human resources (Relevance KEQ 2 and 3, except Telangana) corresponding to required positions due to vacancies at all three levels (state, district and block level). This hinders effective service delivery of SBCC activities and in engagement with community members. 

- Further, it was also found that the capacity of human resources at all three levels in cells of category 1 and 2 to focus on communications activities is limited as priority is given to the governance and programmatic aspects of their professional role. (Efficiency KEQ 2) 

- The human resources at all three levels (state, district and block level) across the six states were at differing levels of competency in regard to knowledge and skills relating to communications and social behavior change. There is a need to ensure uniform level of training and capacity development across all six states to ensure effective service delivery of SBCC activities. 

## **Action points for State:** 

- ➢ The respective state government departments under which the cell is housed should increase advertising of vacant positions at state, district and block level and ensure active hiring to reduce vacancy of said positions. 

- ➢ To ensure that the human resources at all three levels in cells of categories 1 and 2 give equal priority to communications activities and programmatic aspects of their professional role, the state government departments under which the cell is housed should clearly highlight responsibilities related to communications in the job description advertisement. This would decrease the likelihood of these human resources feeling that communications activities are additional responsibilities as compared to programmatic aspects being primary. 

- ➢ Competency mapping - The respective state government departments under which the cell is housed across all six states should conduct a competency mapping exercise in which the current competencies of those human resource who are engaged in communications activities should be mapped against a cadre wise preselected list of required communications related competencies. This mapping would act as a needs assessment exercise from which the level of capacity development required can be determined for human resource who are engaged in communications activities within the state. 

- ➢ Capacity development - The delivery of the capacity development modules and its regular refresher should be taken up by the respective state government departments under which the cell is housed. The conduction should be done by a communications expert hired by the state department. This person should also be trained in workshopping and facilitation to ensure an engaging delivery. This facilitator should work in tandem with the UNICEF consultant placed at the state level. 

## **Action points for UNICEF:** 

- ➢ Development of capacity development framework - UNICEF has the advantage of having subject and technical expertise in methods of SBCC as compared to the state departments which have not fully 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

59 

transitioned from IEC to SBCC. Hence, UNICEF should develop a capacity development framework for the state departments who in turn would be responsible of the conduction and delivery of the same. The framework should provide cadre wise lists of desired knowledge and skills to effectively delivery SBCC activities and meaningfully engage in SBCC programming. Further, the framework should indicate the level of capacity development required as per level of presence/absence of the desired knowledge and skills as assessed in the workforce. 

- ➢ Development of training modules - Basis the capacity development framework; UNICEF should develop training modules which would be differentiated for cadres of human resource engaged in communications activities. Further, the modules can be differentiated on the basis of presence of desired knowledge and skills assessed in the workforce. 

## **Recommendation 5:** 

**Adopt a systematic approach for evidence** Responsible Organization: UNICEF **based SBCC planning** Priority: Medium SBC SS indicator: Information Time frame: 12 - 24  months Recommendation for category: I, II and III 

## **Rationale:** 

- As noted in the effectiveness KEQ 1, the cells in category I and II, and III (WB) have not been able to focus on evidence generation to develop evidence-based communication initiatives due to lack of training and mentorship in required methodologies and HR constraints. The evaluation found instances of evidence-based planning in category III (Telangana) where the communication strategies developed by the CRU are based on secondary data sources. 

- There is a need for planning, designing and implementation of the SBCC strategies, taking due account of local culture, practices and sensibilities 

- As noted in the effectiveness KEQ 7.6, the cell in category I, no evidence of knowledge sharing with allied, collaborating departments and development agencies / NGOs was found in the state. Similarly, across other categories knowledge sharing was not seen as the priority and was need based as in case of COVID -19 pandemic. 

- Similarly, it was found during the evaluation, effectiveness KEQ 7.5, there was little focus on documentation and analysis, and monitoring and evaluation of the SBCC activities beyond the general administrative documentation. This affects SBCC activities due to which strategic thinking and decision making regarding in SBCC programming are negatively affected. 

## **Action points for UNICEF:** 

## **Evidence Generation** 

- ➢ UNICEF through its state level consultant with domain expertise in social behavior change and communication should provide technical expertise in designing appropriate implementation mechanisms for SBCC design and maintain social and behavioral sustainability by planning SBCC by consideration of changing social and behavioral norms and ensuring the needs of varied communities making it gender responsive and equity focused. 

- ➢ To design socially relevant implementation mechanisms for SBCC activities, the state level consultant should work in close collaboration with district level officials through monthly meetings. The district levels officials should provide field level insights on the needs of community members and community representatives. 

- ➢ Similarly, identification of key stakeholder groups (primary – those who are directly addressed, secondarythose who influence primary stakeholders and tertiary- directly or indirectly help or hinder the behaviors of other stakeholders) is crucial for SBCC planning. UNICEF consultant should work in coherence with the district officials to get insights about the social norms and needs of the community and in identification of key stakeholders 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

60 

- ➢ Pre-testing of SBCC material – UNICEF through its state level consultant in collaboration with the department the cell is housed under should conduct the pre-testing of SBCC material to know if the intended audience understands the key messages before finalizing the SBCC material. UNICEF should develop a comprehensive guide for pretesting based on the measures – A) Comprehension – clarity of content, B) Attractiveness C) Acceptance – to the community based on socio-cultural norms, D) Involvement E) Call to action*. Based on the five criteria a tool to be developed for testing of SBCC material through FLWs in the districts where UNICEF has presence. 

- ➢ Pre-testing (data collection) can be carried out by FLWs during the community meetings like VHND. The inputs from the community should be conveyed by the district to the state cell. Finally, by incorporation of the inputs, SBCC material needs to be modified. 

## **Knowledge Management** 

- ➢ UNICEF to institutionalize knowledge management (documentation of good practices, learnings, knowledge sharing) into the overall SBCC planning. 

- ➢ Good practices and lessons learned can be shared across the SBCC cells by publication of knowledge products on yearly basis. Documentation of case studies of successes and challenges and creation of documentation of good practices facilitate knowledge sharing among the state cells. 

- ➢ The SBCC alliance can facilitate an annual conclave for innovative practices in SBCC with participation from MOHFW, state SBCC cells, development partners and other relevant stakeholders which will act as a platform for knowledge sharing and cross learning. 

## **Recommendation 6:** 

|**Ensuring dedicated financial resources for**<br>**SBCC cells**|Responsible Organization: Department under<br>which cell is housed in states and UNICEF|
|---|---|
|Priority: High|SBC SS indicator: Finance|
|Time frame: 6 – 12 months|Recommendation for category: I, II and III|



## **Rationale:** 

During the evaluation, under evaluation criteria of efficiency KEQ 1, it was noted that: 

- ➢ state cell of Assam (category 1) had sufficient funds for certain activities like, RI, a state priority, whereas there were limited financial resources for other SBCC activities beyond RI. 

- ➢ Although, states under category 2 were housed health department, but the state, and district cells had to depend upon financial resources provided by various national programs who collaborated with the cell for SBCC activities. Cell’s efficiency curtailed due to non-availability of earmarked funding for SBCC activities. 

- ➢ CRU (state cell for Telangana) was able to make efficient and effective contribution towards SBCC activities of the state because it was involved in financial planning for the cell. The annual budget for CRU was based upon the strategic plan developed by CRU for the upcoming year, which led to optimum utilization of allocated monetary resources. 

- ➢ Additionally, under evaluation criteria of relevance KEQ 3, it was highlighted in all three categories that there is a major limitation in the state cell’s ability to respond to the needs of community members and representatives due to resource and budget constraints. 

- ➢ Evaluation findings suggest that SBCC cells require independent financial resources for efficient functioning. Efficiency and effectiveness in state cells of states belonging to category 1 and 2 was affected by financial resource constraints as well. Whereas CRU’s financial management system has positively contributed towards efficient functioning of the cell. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

61 

- ➢ It is proposed that the state cell should be given autonomy in deciding its financial resource requirement and utilization mechanism. State governments should be shouldered with the responsibility of providing desired financial resources to cell. 

## **Action points for State:** 

- ➢ State governments should provide cells with opportunities to have an increased agency for conducting independent financial planning and spending the budget on SBCC activities. 

- ➢ States should streamline financial management of the SBCC cells and invite annual planning from the state SBCC cell (in consultation with district cells) and include them in the overall departmental budget of the state to ensure sustainable availability of funds for SBCC activities. 

## **Action point for UNICEF:** 

- ➢ UNICEF should advocate for provisioning of financial resources for SBCC activities in national programs and state interventions. 

## **Recommendation 7:** 

|**Development of SBCC cell repository**|Responsible Organization: Department under<br>which cell is housed in states|
|---|---|
|Priority: Medium|SBC SS indicator: Information|
|Time frame: 6 – 12 months|Recommendation for category: I, II and III|



## **Rationale:** 

- ➢ It was found that it was easier for human resources engaged in communications activities to implement IEC and SBCC activities when they had access to readily available SBCC material (Effectiveness KEQ 4, Category 2). 

- ➢ For example, In UP, communication officials were able to reach out to the district SBCC cell of Balarampur, when in need to access any communication material across aspects of health messaging. The cell had a large reserve of various communication material (posters, pamphlets, flipbooks, videos, etc.) developed by center, state and other development partners. Another example is the district level cells in Mayurbhanj and Puri (Category 2) which served a similar role of a repository for all communications material developed for the district. 

- ➢ The repository should include material which has been contextualized to ensure higher relevance of the material to local needs and norms. For example, in Telangana (Category 3), the CRU adapts national level IEC material into regionally contextual material which includes translation into local languages and changing of imagery. 

## **Action points for State:** 

- ➢ One stop repository of SBCC material: The state SBCC cell should be developed as a one stop repository of SBCC material for accessing and use by programme officials and frontline health workers. At the district level the repository should be physical, whilst at the state level, the repository should be physical and online to ensure greater accessibility to district level officials and frontline health workers. 

- ➢ The repository should contain SBCC material including, but not limited to leaflets, posters, banners, written content on various common topics of public health, flipbooks, charts, designs for wall painting, audio-visual material and other campaign material which are arranged under thematic rubrics. The knowledge resources should be available in English, Hindi and regional languages. 

- ➢ The state instated graphic designer and translator should focus on contextualization of SBCC material to local languages and dialects, culturally relevant imagery and social norms. This will enrich quality of the material stored in the repository. 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

62 

## **Recommendation 8:** 

|**Ensuring monitoring and evaluation of SBCC**<br>**cells**|Responsible Organization: Department under<br>which cell is housed in states and UNICEF|
|---|---|
|Priority: High|SBC SS indicator: Leadership and Governance,<br>Workforce, Service Delivery and Information|
|Time frame: 6 – 12 months|Recommendation for category: I, II and III|



## **Rationale:** 

During the evaluation, under evaluation criteria of effectiveness KEQ 6, it was noted that: 

- Across the categories, monitoring and evaluation of the SBCC cell’s activities was not conducted because there was no mandate and framework for M&E. The cells also lacked either financial resources or human resources (in terms of both people as well as required skill set) to conduct M&E activities. 

- Additionally, inconsistency in effectiveness of the cell in regard to training, documentation etc. could also have been reduced, if there had been a monitoring and evaluation mandate in the programming of the cells. 

- A system for monitoring and evaluation of SBCC activities is critical to ensure regular information flow on performance of activities, deviation of the cell from objectives and overall performance. It is recommended that UNICEF should advocate and provide technical support to states for investing in development of monitoring and evaluation framework. 

## **Action points for State:** 

- ➢ The state SBCC cell should include monitoring and evaluation, as crucial pillar of the SBCC cell for the state. For which the state should instate a domain expert for M and E who can guide other officials for ensuring regular monitoring of SBCC activities in the state. 

- ➢ The state should conduct periodic monitoring and evaluation of the activities of SBCC activities through field visits, community observation etc. 

- ➢ The state should invest in leveraging technology (dashboard, apps etc.) to monitor SBCC activities. 

## **Action points for UNICEF:** 

- ➢ UNICEF should design a sustainable monitoring and evaluation framework involving input and process indicators, like availability of SBCC strategy, percentage of fund utilization, number of training, trainee/trainer feedback etc., for states to follow and adopt in long run. 

- ➢ UNICEF should also act an independent evaluator of the performance of the cells across the states. 

- ➢ UNICEF should train and build capacity of the state officials to conduct monitoring and evaluation activities independently. 

## **Recommendation 9:** 

|**Gender and Equity -**SBCC interventions<br>should be more explicit and strategically<br>integrated to gender equity|Responsible Organization: Respective State<br>Governments with UNICEF’s support|
|---|---|
|Priority: High|SBC SS indicator: Across all Indicators|
|Time frame: 6 – 12 months|Recommendations for Category: I, II, and III|



## **Rationale:** 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

63 

- From the six evaluated states, it was found that although gender and equity are cross-cutting and a critical component of the SBCC, in practice, the Cells in the states have not yet mainstreamed gender equity, and neither are they gender-cognizant while designing SBCC activities, as was noted under relevance and effectiveness across all categories. WASH services and RMNCH+A has given attention to hygiene and sanitation, nutrition and women’s health. 

- However, SBCC activities are largely limited to comprehensive health strategy under RMNCH+A with priority intervention for nutrition, IFA supplementation, and so on. Wide gaps persist in fundamental areas of adolescent sexual and reproductive health (SRH), information and counseling, and in early marriage, teen pregnancy, school dropout of girls, and even male child preference in some states. In this context, the Rashtriya Kishor Swastha Abhijan and ARSH (national programmes for adolescent sexual and reproductive health (SRH) and holistic development) were not featured, neither mentioned by the states during the consultations). 

- As was found across the six evaluated states, there are no (in-depth) gender equity integrated SBCC activities for health for women and men, and adolescent boys and girls. There are no participatory studies related to sexual and reproductive health. Therefore, SBCC activities should emphasize the importance of health priorities but more comprehensively that intersects with related fundamental social issues, central to gender and equity. 

- Furthermore, it was found that the departments are not yet gender-focused, and largely do not have the necessary skills or level of knowledge to initiate gender and equity programmes, except for Odisha which has a relatively better equitable approach to SBCC planning. 

- The evaluation further found that a comprehensive Gender and Equity approach was missing at the state level SBCC agenda, while no specific gender-focused programmes are part of the district cell’s SBCC activities. A gender-transformative approach is thus a critical requirement to address social norms, practices in diverse social and economic contexts by the state and district cells, and assessing areas that need genderbased intervention, such as those in LODOR and other vulnerable communities. In view of this, UNICEF should support to the states in creating an enabling environment that is gender responsive on priority within its next programmatic cycle/ as a part of its transition strategy. Presence of a ‘Gender and Equity aware’ program staff will enable assessment of contexts and consciously address existing gaps, gender constraints, and so on within SBCC. 

- We gather that UNICEF’s support and interventions will be imperative both at state and district levels to initiate a dialogue and consolidate the SBCC agenda on mainstreaming Gender and Equity more thematically within the Cells. To ensure relevant, focused and effective delivery of the SBCC objectives, UNICEF should support state and district cells on research-based inputs on gender responsiveness and inclusivity and create the initial dialogue between states and districts for a Gender Equality Continuum.

In consideration of the review of the common structures mapped across SBCC Cells, the sample states have been clubbed across three layers found below. 

- **1) Assam:** Only state among the six sample states where the SBCC Cell is housed under health dept and institutionalized at the state level. 

- **2)MP, Uttar Pradesh, and Odisha:** All three SBCC Cells focused on health and cross-sectoral themes and institutionalized at state and district levels. 

- **3) West Bengal and Telangana:** Both SBCC Cells are housed outside the health dept and have similar characteristics of SBCC Cell activities focused on health and cross-sectoral themes.

|||Moderators to refrain from making any<br>promises or any commitmentsto the<br>attendees||
|||Moderators tothank all attendeesof the<br>FGDs andrequest for feedbackon the<br>discussion||



## A.13. Study tool – KII 

## **KII with Nodal Leadership, State SBCC Cell Introduction (Can be modified as per interviewer):** 

PricewaterhouseCoopers Private Limited, has been selected as the evaluation team for the ‘Evaluation of UNICEF’s SBCC Cells Programme from 2017-2021’. The evaluation process is divided into three inter-related phases: Inception; data collection, data analysis and reporting; and report writing and dissemination. As a part of the data collection exercise, it is important to have the views of the Nodal Leadership, State SBCC Cell to understand the aspects of the SBCC program that has worked or may have not worked for future course of action. The facilitator can introduce themselves, “I, ______________, am from the evaluation team would like to discuss with you about your engagement with local govt stakeholders and other local workers/leaders in increasing 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

105 

community’s demand and access to basic services such as health, WASH, nutrition, education, protection or otherwise. I am accompanied by__________________, my co-facilitator”.

## **Questions:** 

**Relevance of SBCC Cells-** 

- Can you please share the key milestones in the evolution of the SBCC Cells at the state level? What was the critical support or guidance that you received from UNICEF to be able to operationalize it? What were most critical activities undertaken by the Cell (2017-20) 

- Do you think SBCC Cells are aligned to the flagship programmes and priorities of the national and state governments? Some of the specific areas that we would request you to highlight include its contribution to 

   - Global and national areas of socio-developmental focus (e.g., aspirational districts, high burden districts, SDG). 

   - Your state’s flagship programmes / schemes and the nature of support to integrate SBCC programming in health or development programmes. 

   - The support received in integrating SBCC in emergency response and preparedness (including COVID19 pandemic) 

- Can you please share the structure of the SBCC Cell and how it is embedded in the district’s Departmental structure? Please share the roles and responsibilities that the SBCC cell has that complement’s the Department’s activities. 

- How does the SBCC Cell respond to the needs of community members and community representatives (FLWs, trainees etc.) in your state? Does it address the local issues with regards to gender and equity? If yes, please share how this is possible. 

- According to you, how has UNICEF been able to support the SBCC cell in adapting/ responding to the evolving needs (legislations/ schemes / emerging evidence) of the government especially in the covid times? Please share examples of the same 

## **Effectiveness of SBCC cells** 

- IEC has been a more traditional method used in social communication. According to you, how effective has the cell been in transforming the dialogue towards SBCC in your state and district?  Please share some examples of this shift with reference to initiatives before 2017 and after. 

- Have the SBCC Cells successfully fulfilled their role as institutions for identifying and addressing challenges, supporting evidence generation, strategic planning, operationalization, monitoring, analysis, training, documentation, knowledge sharing? 

- How has the institutionalization of SBCC-based programming through the setting up of SBCC Cells strengthened the health system (please refer to the six pillars)? Has it improved collaborations, partnerships, and convergence with allied departments at the national and state level/ Developmental agencies/ corporates?

106 

- How effective is the communication strategy, material and content developed by the SBCC Cells towards demand generation for development schemes and initiatives?  Please share examples of strategies which reflected improved demand generation in the last five years. Is there any evidence or document to support the same? 

## **Efficiency of the SBCC cells:** 

- Did the SBCC Cells efficiently deliver/implement its activities (resource planning including human resources, allocation, spending, monitoring, and mobilization of resources) and function within the planned/allocated monetary resources? 

- Are there any special initiatives taken to improve efficiency of operations of SBCC Cells? Please mention the same 

- In your opinion, what were the desired results from the Cell? Any specific constraints that have led to the disruption of the Cell to function as planned? 

## **Sustainability of SBCC cells:** 

1. In which ways do you think state departments have been able to integrate SBCC in the planning, implementation, and monitoring of key programmes? Please share a few examples. 

2. Does the government (at the state and district-level) have the capacity (with respect to human resources, governance mechanism and ownership within the system) to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently? 

3. According to you, has the Cell established partnerships to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently? 

## **Coherence of SBCC cells:** 

1. How do the activities/interventions of the SBCC Cells      synergies and/ or complement      the existing programme/initiatives of national/state government, non-governmental partners, and other sector players? Please share if there are joint programmes to reflect this. Are there MoUs to support collaboration and detailing the roles of the agencies. Please share the same. 

2. What are some of the challenges in integrating SBCC cells in state programmes in enhancing the programme uptake of government schemes across sectors and in achieving improved outcomes at the programme level? Please share your recommendations to improve the integration of SBCC in state programmes. 

## **Way Forward** 

1. In your opinion, what has worked the best and what can work better? 

2. What are the forward-looking vision and goals aimed for the SBCC Cell for the coming years? 

3. What would be your recommendations in replicating this model of SBCC Programming for other states and regions?

## **Relevance of SBCC Cells-** 

1. Can you please share the ways in which SBCC Cells have been relevant to the flagship programmes and priorities of the national and state governments? Some of the specific areas that we would request you to highlight include its contribution to: 

- Global and national areas of socio-developmental focus (e.g., aspirational districts, high burden districts, SDG). 

- Your state’s flagship programmes/ schemes at the national and state level. 

- The nature of support to integrate SBCC programming in health or development programmes. 

- The support received in integrating SBCC in emergency response and preparedness. (Including COVID19 pandemic) 

2. How relevant, appropriate, and aligned is the design of the SBCC Cell to the State's established systems and institutions of service delivery? Particularly with respect to the existing state-specific institutional setups and structures? 

3. In what ways does the SBCC Cell respond to the needs of community members and community representatives (FLWs, trainees etc.) Does it adequately address the local issues with regards to gender and equity? Please share how this has been possible.

115 

4. How has UNICEF been able to support the SBCC cell in adapting/ responding to the evolving needs (legislations/ schemes / emerging evidence) of the government especially during Covid? 

## **Effectiveness of SBCC cells** 

1. According to you, what are the ways in which the setting up of SBCC Cells has strengthened government systems in bringing about more effective and evidence-based communication initiatives? How have the programmes and initiatives (supported by SBCC Cells) incorporated evidence-based communication programming for behavioral change and demand generation? 

2. Are stakeholders equipped with skills to plan, implement, monitor behavioral change, and demand generation initiatives through capacity building? 

3. Have SBCC Cells successfully supported integration of a social and gender equity and inclusion approach for evidence generation and SBCC programming? 

4. IEC has been a more traditional method used in social communication. How effective has the cell been in transforming the dialogue towards SBCC? 

5. Have the SBCC Cells successfully fulfilled their role as institutions for identifying and add ressing challenges, supporting evidence generation, strategic planning, operationalization, monitoring, analysis, training, documentation, knowledge sharing, including convening partnerships? 

6. How has the institutionalization of SBCC-based programming through the setting up of SBCC Cells strengthened the health system? Has it improved collaborations, partnerships, and convergence with allied departments at the national and state level/ Developmental agencies/ corporates? 

7. How effective is the communication strategy, material and content developed by the SBCC Cells towards demand generation for development schemes and initiatives? 

## **Efficiency of the SBCC cells:** 

1. Did the SBCC Cells efficiently deliver/implement its activities (resource planning including human resources, allocation, mobilization. spending, monitoring, and mobilization of resources) and function within the planned/allocated funding? 

2. Are there any special initiatives taken to improve efficiency in the operations of SBCC Cells? 

3. How are timelines planned for SBCC activities? Are they contextualized to the local scenario and consider dependencies? 

## **Sustainability of SBCC cells:** 

1. Do you think the other state departments have been able to integrate SBCC in the planning, implementation, and monitoring of key programmes? Has this been integrated in their budgets and is it adequate? Please share a few examples. 

2. Does the government (at the state and district-level) have the capacity (with respect to human resources, governance mechanism and ownership within the system) to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently? 

3. Do you think the Cell has established partnerships to successfully carry out the activities of the SBCC Cells independently? 

4. Has SBCC programming been integrated within the budgeting cycle at the state/district/ block level? 

**5.** Does the Cell receive an allocated budget? How much percentage of the Cell functions are adequately covered under the budget received? 

## **Way Forward** 

1. In your opinion, what worked the best and what can work better? 

2. What are the forward-looking vision and goals aimed for the SBCC Cell for the coming years? 

3. What would be your recommendations in replicating this model of SBCC Programming for other states and regions?

## B. Study tool - FGD 

## **FGD with District SBCC Coordinator** 

## **Introduction (Can be modified as per interviewer):** 

PricewaterhouseCoopers Private Limited, has been selected as the evaluation team for the ‘Evaluation of UNICEF’s SBCC Cells Programme from 2017-2021’. The evaluation process is divided into three inter-related phases: Inception; data collection, data analysis and reporting; and report writing and dissemination. As a part of the data collection exercise, it is important to have the views of the District SBCC Coordinators to understand the aspects of the SBCC strategy that has worked or may have not worked for a future course of action.  The facilitator can introduce themselves, “I, ______________, am from the evaluation team would like to discuss with you about your communication with local stakeholders particularly AWW/ ASHA and other local workers/leaders in increasing community’s demand and access to basic services such as health, WASH, nutrition, education, protection or otherwise. I am accompanied by__________________, my co-facilitator”. 

Thank the participants for joining the discussion and share that this is meant for collective discussion and not to capture individual views. Thus **, no personal names will be reflected in the report** .  It is also important to indicate that the FGD would take about one hour of their time. As a process, the facilitator will raise the issue of discussion and the participants would share their knowledge, views, perceptions, and thoughts. It is important to give the participants the confidence that **they can express their views without fear, and they would not be quoted or forced to participate at any point of time and their participation is entirely voluntary** . **Request permission to record the interaction for documentation purposes. All information and responses shared during the discussion would remain confidential and anonymous. All the evaluators would follow the ‘do no harm principle’ in conducting the discussion.** 

Before posing the specific questions, the facilitator allows participants to introduce themselves; particularly their role and department or programme they are associated with and for how long. 

## **Pointers for discussion:** 

1. What is your view about the SBCC Cell and how has it evolved over the years? Are there any specific examples that describe or explain the evolution of BCC activities at the district level? 

2. What according to you are the most important roles of SBCC Cell at the district level or SBCC activities undertaken at the district level? (As applicable depending on the categorization of the state). Please indicate if the District Cells are focused on the activities indicated in supporting evidence generation, strategic planning, operationalization, monitoring, analysis, training, documentation, knowledge sharing and building partnerships). 

3. Please share the specific roles and responsibilities that you have in executing the SBCC activities at the district level. The reporting framework, any changes in the last five years as compared to the past. 

4. The complementary role with respect to the Departmental positions at the district level. Are there any overlaps or what is the mechanism of coordinating with stakeholders at various levels e.g., state SBCC Cell, District functionaries and with other functionaries below? Also indicate if there is a structure in place for the same. 

5. What are the various campaigns and SBCC activities undertaken at the state level (such as demand generation for RI, health, nutrition, and WASH services)? What were the themes taken up in the last five years (2017-22), has there been any shift in those in the recent times? Are they also aligned to the needs of the community at the local level? 

UNICEF SBCC Cells Programme Evaluation for 2017-21 PwC 

124 

6. Please share the mechanism that is undertaken to decide on the theme and the content of the BCC strategy or activities, if evidence or research or MIS data is referred to decide. Please indicate if the planners consider evidence for incremental increase in proportion of women and vulnerable groups targeted/reached through mix-media communication 

7. Has there been any collaboration, partnerships and convergence with other departments, NGOs, and other agencies to implement the SBCC activities at the district or sub district level? Have such collaborations helped in thematic exchange, resource sharing, coverage, and any other benefits? 

8. What are the challenges you face in actualizing communication strategies in the field through various departmental workforce? How do you mitigate the same? 

9. What supervisory or technical support do you require to design and deliver SBCC activities? What additional support would help you? 

10. Request insights to make the SBCC campaigns and strategies more localized and equitable (Suggested areas:  domestic violence; child marriage)
